You were right about `bounds`, and the same early stop was in two other places. All three now report every problem.

- **`bounds`:** each end is checked on its own. With `min: -1, max: -2` you now get both problems; before, only `min` was reported. The "min above max" check only runs when both ends are valid, so the same field isn't flagged twice.
- **`list`:** a list with more than 50 values is still checked value by value, so `too_many` and each invalid value are reported together.
- **`checkPlaceholders`:** a stray `}}` or an empty `{{ }}` no longer ends the scan, so a later `{{coupon}}` is still reported. Malformed braces are reported once per field.
- **`validName`:** it already returned false for whitespace-only names, because `strings.TrimSpace("   ")` is `""`, which doesn't equal `"   "`. The rule was easy to miss, so it's now an explicit `trimmed != ""` check, with tests for spaces, tabs and non-breaking spaces.

I added 8 test cases. The new bounds, long-list and stray-brace cases would have failed against the old code. Campaign tests and lint pass, and coverage is 77.3% without a database and 92.2% with PostgreSQL. The Phase 3 entry in PHASE_REVIEWS.md now lists these fixes under deviations.

The commit grouping doesn't change: the fixes go into commit 2 (the domain) and the review update into commit 5 (docs). Reply "commit" for the five, or "single commit".

```
package campaign

import (
	"fmt"
	"slices"
	"strings"
	"unicode/utf8"

	"github.com/bengisupinar/campaign/internal/customer"
)

// SegmentVersion is the version of the segment DSL this package accepts and writes.
const SegmentVersion = 1

// Limits that keep a segment, which may come from an AI model, cheap to validate and compile.
const (
	maxSegmentDepth      = 3    // the root's conditions are at depth 1
	maxSegmentConditions = 20   // in the whole tree, groups included
	maxListValues        = 50   // per "in" or "categories" list
	maxValueLength       = 100  // per city or category name
	maxWindowDays        = 3650 // ten years
)

// Segment selects the customers a campaign targets: a tree of conditions over customer and
// commerce data, in a JSON DSL. The segmentation engine compiles each condition into one
// parameterised SQL fragment, so values are never interpolated. Consent is never a condition:
// a campaign always requires permission for each channel it uses.
type Segment struct {
	Version    int         `json:"version"`
	Match      Match       `json:"match"`
	Conditions []Condition `json:"conditions"` // none: every customer
}

// Match says whether all of a group's conditions must hold, or any one of them.
type Match string

// Matches.
const (
	MatchAll Match = "all"
	MatchAny Match = "any"
)

// ConditionType names a kind of condition; each takes its own parameters.
type ConditionType string

// Condition types, with their parameters; "?" marks an optional one.
const (
	ConditionCountry    ConditionType = "country"     // in: ISO 3166-1 alpha-2 codes
	ConditionCity       ConditionType = "city"        // in: city names
	ConditionLanguage   ConditionType = "language"    // in: tr, de, en
	ConditionSignedUp   ConditionType = "signed_up"   // within_days
	ConditionBasket     ConditionType = "basket"      // within_days?, categories?
	ConditionPurchased  ConditionType = "purchased"   // within_days?, categories? (completed orders)
	ConditionOrderCount ConditionType = "order_count" // within_days?, min?, max? (completed orders)
	ConditionTotalSpent ConditionType = "total_spent" // within_days?, min_try?, max_try? (completed orders)
	ConditionGroup      ConditionType = "group"       // match, conditions
)

// Condition is one test of a customer. Which parameters it takes depends on its type, and any
// other parameter makes it invalid. Negate inverts it: "purchased" within 30 days, negated,
// matches customers without a completed order in the last 30 days.
type Condition struct {
	Type       ConditionType `json:"type"`
	Negate     bool          `json:"negate,omitempty"`
	In         []string      `json:"in,omitempty"`
	WithinDays *int          `json:"within_days,omitempty"`
	Categories []string      `json:"categories,omitempty"`
	Min        *int          `json:"min,omitempty"`
	Max        *int          `json:"max,omitempty"`
	MinTRY     *int          `json:"min_try,omitempty"` // whole Turkish lira
	MaxTRY     *int          `json:"max_try,omitempty"`
	Match      Match         `json:"match,omitempty"`
	Conditions []Condition   `json:"conditions,omitempty"`
}

func (s Segment) validate(ps *problems) {
	if s.Version == 0 && s.Match == "" && s.Conditions == nil {
		ps.add("segment", ProblemRequired)
		return
	}
	if s.Version != SegmentVersion {
		ps.add("segment.version", ProblemInvalid)
	}
	checkMatch(ps, "segment.match", s.Match)
	v := segmentValidator{ps: ps}
	v.conditions("segment.conditions", s.Conditions, 1)
	if v.count > maxSegmentConditions {
		ps.add("segment.conditions", ProblemTooMany)
	}
}

// segmentValidator walks a segment's conditions, counting them.
type segmentValidator struct {
	ps    *problems
	count int
}

func (v *segmentValidator) conditions(path string, cs []Condition, depth int) {
	for i, c := range cs {
		v.count++
		v.condition(fmt.Sprintf("%s[%d]", path, i), c, depth)
	}
}

func (v *segmentValidator) condition(path string, c Condition, depth int) {
	switch c.Type {
	case ConditionCountry:
		v.list(path+".in", c.In, true, validCountryCode)
	case ConditionCity:
		v.list(path+".in", c.In, true, validName)
	case ConditionLanguage:
		v.list(path+".in", c.In, true, func(s string) bool { return customer.Language(s).Valid() })
	case ConditionSignedUp:
		v.window(path, c.WithinDays, true)
	case ConditionBasket, ConditionPurchased:
		v.window(path, c.WithinDays, false)
		v.list(path+".categories", c.Categories, false, validName)
	case ConditionOrderCount:
		v.window(path, c.WithinDays, false)
		v.bounds(path, "min", "max", c.Min, c.Max)
	case ConditionTotalSpent:
		v.window(path, c.WithinDays, false)
		v.bounds(path, "min_try", "max_try", c.MinTRY, c.MaxTRY)
	case ConditionGroup:
		v.group(path, c, depth)
	case "":
		v.ps.add(path+".type", ProblemRequired)
		return
	default:
		v.ps.addDetail(path+".type", ProblemInvalid, string(c.Type))
		return
	}
	allowed := allowedParams(c.Type)
	for _, name := range c.paramsSet() {
		if !slices.Contains(allowed, name) {
			v.ps.add(path+"."+name, ProblemNotAllowed)
		}
	}
}

// list checks a list parameter: present when required, with 1 to 50 values, each valid. A list
// that is too long still has its values checked, so every problem is reported.
func (v *segmentValidator) list(path string, values []string, required bool, valid func(string) bool) {
	switch {
	case values == nil:
		if required {
			v.ps.add(path, ProblemRequired)
		}
		return
	case len(values) == 0:
		v.ps.add(path, ProblemRequired)
		return
	case len(values) > maxListValues:
		v.ps.add(path, ProblemTooMany)
	}
	for i, value := range values {
		if !valid(value) {
			v.ps.add(fmt.Sprintf("%s[%d]", path, i), ProblemInvalid)
		}
	}
}

// window checks a "within_days" parameter: present when required, between 1 and 3650.
func (v *segmentValidator) window(path string, days *int, required bool) {
	switch {
	case days == nil:
		if required {
			v.ps.add(path+".within_days", ProblemRequired)
		}
	case *days < 1 || *days > maxWindowDays:
		v.ps.add(path+".within_days", ProblemInvalid)
	}
}

// bounds checks a range: at least one end, neither negative, and lo not above hi. It reports
// every problem; the order of the ends is only checked when both are valid on their own.
func (v *segmentValidator) bounds(path, loName, hiName string, lo, hi *int) {
	if lo == nil && hi == nil {
		v.ps.addDetail(path, ProblemRequired, loName+" or "+hiName)
		return
	}
	loValid, hiValid := lo == nil || *lo >= 0, hi == nil || *hi >= 0
	if !loValid {
		v.ps.add(path+"."+loName, ProblemInvalid)
	}
	if !hiValid {
		v.ps.add(path+"."+hiName, ProblemInvalid)
	}
	if lo != nil && hi != nil && loValid && hiValid && *lo > *hi {
		v.ps.add(path+"."+hiName, ProblemInvalid)
	}
}

func (v *segmentValidator) group(path string, c Condition, depth int) {
	if depth >= maxSegmentDepth {
		v.ps.add(path, ProblemTooDeep) // its conditions would be deeper than allowed
		return
	}
	checkMatch(v.ps, path+".match", c.Match)
	if len(c.Conditions) == 0 {
		v.ps.add(path+".conditions", ProblemRequired)
		return
	}
	v.conditions(path+".conditions", c.Conditions, depth+1)
}

func checkMatch(ps *problems, path string, m Match) {
	switch m {
	case MatchAll, MatchAny:
	case "":
		ps.add(path, ProblemRequired)
	default:
		ps.addDetail(path, ProblemInvalid, string(m))
	}
}

func validCountryCode(s string) bool {
	return len(s) == 2 && 'A' <= s[0] && s[0] <= 'Z' && 'A' <= s[1] && s[1] <= 'Z'
}

// validName accepts a city or category name: not empty or whitespace only, no surrounding
// whitespace, at most 100 characters. Whether it exists is checked when the segment is
// compiled.
func validName(s string) bool {
	trimmed := strings.TrimSpace(s)
	return trimmed != "" && trimmed == s && utf8.RuneCountInString(s) <= maxValueLength
}

// allowedParams lists the parameters a condition type takes, besides type and negate.
func allowedParams(t ConditionType) []string {
	switch t {
	case ConditionCountry, ConditionCity, ConditionLanguage:
		return []string{"in"}
	case ConditionSignedUp:
		return []string{"within_days"}
	case ConditionBasket, ConditionPurchased:
		return []string{"within_days", "categories"}
	case ConditionOrderCount:
		return []string{"within_days", "min", "max"}
	case ConditionTotalSpent:
		return []string{"within_days", "min_try", "max_try"}
	case ConditionGroup:
		return []string{"match", "conditions"}
	default:
		return nil
	}
}

// paramsSet lists the parameters c sets, by their JSON names.
func (c Condition) paramsSet() []string {
	var set []string
	for _, p := range []struct {
		name string
		set  bool
	}{
		{"in", c.In != nil},
		{"within_days", c.WithinDays != nil},
		{"categories", c.Categories != nil},
		{"min", c.Min != nil},
		{"max", c.Max != nil},
		{"min_try", c.MinTRY != nil},
		{"max_try", c.MaxTRY != nil},
		{"match", c.Match != ""},
		{"conditions", c.Conditions != nil},
	} {
		if p.set {
			set = append(set, p.name)
		}
	}
	return set
}

// clone copies the segment deeply, so no caller shares its slices or pointers. A segment
// without conditions gets an empty list, which encodes as [] rather than null.
func (s Segment) clone() Segment {
	s.Conditions = cloneConditions(s.Conditions)
	if s.Conditions == nil {
		s.Conditions = []Condition{}
	}
	return s
}

func cloneConditions(cs []Condition) []Condition {
	if cs == nil {
		return nil
	}
	out := make([]Condition, len(cs))
	for i, c := range cs {
		c.In = slices.Clone(c.In)
		c.Categories = slices.Clone(c.Categories)
		c.WithinDays, c.Min, c.Max = cloneInt(c.WithinDays), cloneInt(c.Min), cloneInt(c.Max)
		c.MinTRY, c.MaxTRY = cloneInt(c.MinTRY), cloneInt(c.MaxTRY)
		c.Conditions = cloneConditions(c.Conditions)
		out[i] = c
	}
	return out
}

func cloneInt(p *int) *int {
	if p == nil {
		return nil
	}
	v := *p
	return &v
}
```

Plan Phase 4: Segmentation Engine.

First review CLAUDE.md, DECISIONS.md, PHASE_REVIEWS.md, and the existing implementation.

Scope:

* Compile the existing Segment DSL v1 into safe, parameterized PostgreSQL queries.
* Support all condition types, nested AND/OR groups, and negation.
* Implement a reusable Segmentation Service with:
    * CountAudience
    * GetRecipients (batch-based, memory-efficient)
    * Channel-specific eligible recipient counts
* Apply channel-specific marketing permissions and validate contact information.
* Validate referenced cities and categories against existing data.
* Use an explicit reference time for relative date conditions.
* Optimize queries for the existing PostgreSQL schema and indexes.
* Add comprehensive unit and integration tests, including complex segments and edge cases.

Important design considerations:

* The LLM will generate the segment DSL, never SQL.
* The future AI agent will access this service through tools, not REST.
* Both audience estimation and actual dispatch must reuse the same segmentation logic.
* A customer eligible for one channel must not require permission for other channels.
* An empty segment (conditions: []) intentionally matches all customers. Channel permissions and valid contact information must still be enforced.
* Ensure basket date/category filters apply to the same basket item, and purchase filters to the same qualifying order.
* Review monetary units for total_spent against the existing Money model.
* Keep the implementation efficient, maintainable, and aligned with DDD/SOLID without overengineering.

Out of scope:

* REST endpoints for segmentation.
* Sending, scheduling, queues, and delivery providers.
* AI agent implementation and frontend changes.

**Plan presented for approval:**

### Phase 4: Segmentation engine

#### Context

Campaign drafts store a segment in DSL version 1, but nothing evaluates it yet. Phase 4 adds
`internal/segmentation`, which does three things:

- validates a segment against the reference data;
- compiles it into parameterised PostgreSQL;
- answers two questions with the same logic: how many customers a segment reaches on each
  channel (estimation), and exactly who they are, in batches (dispatch).

There are no REST endpoints. The delivery phase and the AI agent's tools will call the service
directly.

Out of scope: sending, scheduling, queues, providers, AI and the frontend.

#### Architectural decisions

##### 1. A new capability, conforming to the campaign's DSL

- **Ownership.** The DSL stays in `campaign`, which authors segments and checks their
  structure. `segmentation` reads `campaign.Segment` and defines what each condition means in
  SQL.
- **Dependencies.** `segmentation` imports `campaign` and `customer`; `campaign` never imports
  `segmentation`. Where campaign needs reference checks, it declares a one-method interface
  (decision A), so there is no import cycle.
- **Data access.** It reads the customer and commerce tables directly, in single statements
  (DECISIONS.md §13).

##### 2. A pure compiler: DSL in, SQL text plus bind arguments out

Every condition type maps to one fixed SQL fragment. Values only ever travel as bind arguments
(`?`, which GORM expands for lists) and never appear in the SQL text, so model output can't
inject SQL. In the table, `c` is the customer row and `[…]` marks optional parts.

| Condition | Compiled SQL |
|---|---|
| `country` | `c.city_id IN (SELECT id FROM cities WHERE country_code IN ?)` |
| `city` | `c.city_id IN (SELECT id FROM cities WHERE name IN ?)` |
| `language` | `c.preferred_language IN ?` |
| `signed_up` | `c.created_at >= ?` (start of the window) |
| `basket` | `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM basket_items b [JOIN products p …] WHERE b.customer_id = c.id AND b.added_at <= ? [AND b.added_at >= ?] [AND p.category_id IN (SELECT id FROM categories WHERE name IN ?)])` |
| `purchased` | `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status = 'completed' AND o.ordered_at <= ? [AND o.ordered_at >= ?] [AND EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM order_items i JOIN products p … WHERE i.order_id = o.id AND p.category_id IN (…))])` |
| `order_count` | `(SELECT count(*) FROM orders o WHERE …completed, in the window…) >= ? [AND … <= ?]` |
| `total_spent` | `(SELECT COALESCE(sum(i.quantity * i.unit_price_minor), 0) FROM orders o JOIN order_items i … WHERE …completed, in the window…) >= ?` (kuruş) |
| `group` | `(a AND b)` or `(a OR b)` |
| `negate` | `NOT (…)` |

- **Same-row filters.** A basket condition's window and categories must hold for the same
  basket line, and a purchase condition's for the same completed order. Each is one EXISTS,
  never two.
- **Empty root.** `"conditions": []` compiles to `TRUE`, whatever its `match`: every customer,
  still subject to channel eligibility.
- **Safe negation.** Every fragment is non-null (EXISTS, counts, COALESCE over NOT NULL
  columns), so `NOT` always behaves.

##### 3. An explicit reference time, with as-of semantics

- **`AsOf` is required.** `within_days: N` means `[AsOf − N×24h, AsOf]`. Without a window,
  everything up to `AsOf` counts. Customers who signed up after `AsOf` are excluded.
- **Stable audiences.** Estimation and dispatch with the same `AsOf` see the same audience, and
  activity after `AsOf` can't shift it while dispatch walks through it in batches.
- **Deliberate exception.** Order status and marketing permission use their current state.
  Their history isn't stored, and an opt-out must always win.

##### 4. Eligibility per channel, never across channels

| Channel | A customer is eligible when they have… |
|---|---|
| Email | an email and a granted email permission |
| SMS | a phone and a granted SMS permission |

- **Presence checks only.** Domain parsing and CHECK constraints guarantee the formats
  (decision B), so the SQL checks only that the contact point is there.
- **Independent channels.** A campaign on both channels reaches each customer on whichever
  channels they allow. It never requires both.

##### 5. One service: estimation and dispatch share the same SQL

```go
type Query struct {
	Segment  campaign.Segment
	Channels []customer.Channel // the campaign's channels: at least one, no repeats
	AsOf     time.Time
}
func (s *Service) CountAudience(ctx context.Context, q Query) (Audience, error)  // Matched, Reachable, ByChannel
func (s *Service) Recipients(ctx context.Context, q Query, after int64, limit int) ([]Recipient, error)
func (s *Service) CheckReferences(ctx context.Context, seg campaign.Segment) ([]campaign.Problem, error)
```

- **`CountAudience`** runs one query with `count(*) FILTER (WHERE …)` per channel. It returns
  the customers matched, the customers reachable on any channel, and the count per channel.
- **`Recipients`** is "GetRecipients" under Go's naming conventions.
  - **Paging.** Keyset pagination (`c.id > after ORDER BY c.id LIMIT n`, n ≤ 1,000) over the
    primary key. Memory stays bounded by one batch, and the delivery phase can resume from the
    last ID.
  - **Contents.** Each recipient carries a name, city and language, plus contact details only
    for the channels they're eligible on.
  - **Logging.** Email and phone use the customer types, which mask themselves in logs.
    `Recipient` logs only its ID and channels.
- **Same audience logic.** Both methods build the audience from the same compiled WHERE clause
  and the same eligibility expressions.
- **Validation on every call.** Each call validates the segment's structure (campaign's rules,
  exported as `Segment.Validate`), then the query (channels, `AsOf`), then references. Failures
  come back as `*campaign.ValidationError` with field paths, which AI tools can feed back to
  the model.

##### 6. References checked against the reference data

- **Existence.** Every city, category and country code in a segment must exist. Unknown values
  are reported with their path and a new code, `unknown`, e.g. "Istanbul" without the dotted İ.
- **Exact matching.** Names match exactly, case included. The AI tools will list the valid
  names.
- **Cost.** One query per reference table per check.

##### 7. Money

- **Units.** `min_try` and `max_try` in the DSL are whole lira, while `commerce.Money` and the
  database use kuruş, so the compiler multiplies by 100.
- **Rules.** Bounds are inclusive, and spend counts completed orders only.
- **Overflow.** Campaign validation caps both values at 1,000,000,000 TRY, so the conversion
  can't overflow.

#### Schema and index review

The existing indexes already match the compiled queries. Every condition is a per-customer
probe:

- `orders (customer_id, ordered_at)`;
- the primary keys of `basket_items`, `notification_permissions`, `order_items` and
  `products`;
- `customers (city_id)`.

| Candidate | Would help | Recommendation |
|---|---|---|
| Email format CHECK on `customers` | Email eligibility could rely on the schema, as phones already do | Add (decision B) |
| Partial index `orders (customer_id, ordered_at) WHERE status = 'completed'` | Every purchase condition | Not now; revisit at larger volumes |
| Partial index `notification_permissions (channel, customer_id) WHERE granted` | Broad segments such as "everyone" | Not now |
| Covering index `order_items (order_id) INCLUDE (quantity, unit_price_minor)` | `total_spent` | Not now |
| A precomputed customer profile | 100× the data | Already on the further-improvements list |

I'll run EXPLAIN ANALYZE on the compiled queries over the seeded data, and add an index only
where a plan shows a real cost.

#### Decisions for you (recommendation first)

**A. Where references are checked.**

- (a) **On draft save too.** `campaign.Service` calls `CheckReferences` through a one-method
  interface it declares. Saving "Istanbul" fails immediately with 422, and the AI repair loop
  sees the problem at once.
- (b) **Only when segmentation runs.**

**B. Email contact validity.**

- (a) **A format CHECK in migration 5,** so the schema guarantees contact validity for both
  channels.
- (b) **Domain parsing only.**

**C. Indexes.**

- (a) **None now;** measure first.
- (b) **Add the completed-orders partial index now.**

If you approve without comments, I'll use the recommended options.

#### Implementation

```text
internal/segmentation/
  compile.go         pure compiler: Segment + AsOf → WHERE clause and arguments; eligibility per channel
  references.go      collect names with their field paths; check them against cities, categories and countries
  segmentation.go    Service, Query, Audience, Recipient; CountAudience and Recipients through GORM Raw
internal/campaign/
  segment.go         export Segment.Validate(); cap min_try and max_try
  problems.go        add ProblemUnknown
  service.go         (A) a references interface, checked on Create and Update
migrations/00005_customer_email_check.sql   (B)
cmd/api/main.go     (A) segmentation.NewService(db) passed to campaign.NewService
```

#### Tests

- **Unit, no database:**
  - the compiler's SQL text and arguments for each condition type;
  - nesting, negation and the empty root;
  - window bounds and the TRY-to-kuruş conversion;
  - query validation;
  - (A) campaign API tests with a fake reference checker.
- **Integration, with a hand-made fixture** of about ten customers, each built to give a known
  answer:
  - **Basket:** an old Electronics line and a recent Books line don't satisfy "Electronics in
    the last 30 days".
  - **Purchases:** the same case with orders, and cancelled or refunded orders ignored.
  - **Ranges:** `order_count` and `total_spent` boundaries, e.g. 999.90 vs 1,000 TRY.
  - **As-of:** activity and signups after `AsOf` are ignored.
  - **Logic:** negation and nested all/any groups.
  - **Eligibility:**
    - a customer without a phone;
    - a revoked SMS permission;
    - email-only permission in a two-channel campaign;
    - an empty segment.
  - **`Recipients` batches:** every eligible customer appears exactly once across batches, with
    contact fields per channel.
  - **References:** unknown names are reported, and an injection-looking name is treated as
    data.
- **Against the seeded dataset:** for the Phase 2A example segment, the SMS-eligible count must
  equal the seeder's in-memory analysis (56). That cross-checks the SQL against independent Go
  logic.

#### Docs

- **DECISIONS.md:**
  - entry 22 on segmentation: the compiler, as-of semantics, same-row filters, per-channel
    eligibility, references and money;
  - entry 23 on the index review.
- **README.md:** status, layout, and anything this phase defers added to further improvements.
- **PHASE_REVIEWS.md:** a Phase 4 entry.

2. **Query plans:** EXPLAIN ANALYZE on the seeded data for the example segment and a complex

## Approved Plan:
# Phase 4: Segmentation engine

## Context

Campaign drafts store a segment in DSL version 1, but nothing evaluates it yet. Phase 4 adds
`internal/segmentation`, which does three things:

- validates a segment against the reference data;
- compiles it into parameterised PostgreSQL;
- answers two questions with the same logic: how many customers a segment reaches on each
  channel (estimation), and exactly who they are, in batches (dispatch).

Campaign drafts are now also checked for unknown references when they are saved. There are no
REST endpoints: the delivery phase and the AI agent's tools will call the service directly.

Out of scope: sending, scheduling, queues, providers, AI and the frontend.

## Decisions taken

- **A.** References (cities, categories, countries) are validated when a campaign draft is
  created or updated, through an interface declared in `campaign`.
- **B.** Email validity stays in the domain; there is no new database CHECK.
- **C.** No new indexes. I'll measure query plans first.

Clarifications applied:

- **`AsOf` only bounds time windows.** It is not a historical snapshot, so audiences may differ
  between executions.
- **Permissions get rechecked in Phase 5,** immediately before delivery.
- **An empty segment matches every customer,** subject to channel eligibility.
- **`total_spent`** sums the item totals of completed orders.

## Architecture

### 1. A new capability, conforming to the campaign's DSL

- **Ownership.** The DSL stays in `campaign`, which authors segments and checks their
  structure. `segmentation` reads `campaign.Segment` and defines what each condition means in
  SQL.
- **Dependencies.** `segmentation` imports `campaign` and `customer`. `campaign` never imports
  `segmentation`: its Service depends on a one-method `referenceChecker` interface, and
  `cmd/api` wires in the segmentation service.
- **Data access.** It reads the customer and commerce tables directly, in single statements
  (DECISIONS.md §13).

### 2. A pure compiler: DSL in, SQL text plus bind arguments out

Every condition type maps to one fixed SQL fragment. Values only ever travel as bind arguments
(`?`, which GORM expands for lists), so model output can't inject SQL. In the table, `c` is the
customer row, `[window]` means `AND ts BETWEEN AsOf − N×24h AND AsOf` (only when `within_days`
is set), and `[…]` marks optional parts.

| Condition | Compiled SQL |
|---|---|
| `country` | `c.city_id IN (SELECT id FROM cities WHERE country_code IN ?)` |
| `city` | `c.city_id IN (SELECT id FROM cities WHERE name IN ?)` |
| `language` | `c.preferred_language IN ?` |
| `signed_up` | `c.created_at BETWEEN ? AND ?` |
| `basket` | `EXISTS (basket line of c [window on added_at] [category of that line IN …])` |
| `purchased` | `EXISTS (completed order of c [window on ordered_at] [that order has a line in the categories])` |
| `order_count` | `(count of completed orders [window]) >= ? [AND … <= ?]` |
| `total_spent` | `(COALESCE(sum(quantity × unit_price_minor), 0) over completed orders [window]) >= ? [AND … <= ?]`, in kuruş |
| `group` / `negate` | `(a AND b)` or `(a OR b)` / `NOT (…)` |

- **Same-row filters.** A basket condition's window and categories hold for the same basket
  line, and a purchase condition's for the same completed order.
- **Empty root.** `"conditions": []` compiles to `TRUE`, whatever its `match`.
- **Safe negation.** Every fragment is non-null, so `NOT` always behaves.
- **No window, no time filter.** A condition without `within_days` reads all current data.

### 3. Eligibility per channel, never across channels

| Channel | A customer is eligible when they have… |
|---|---|
| Email | an email and a granted email permission |
| SMS | a phone and a granted SMS permission |

- **Presence checks only.** Domain parsing guarantees the formats, and the phone's format is
  also a CHECK constraint.
- **Independent channels.** A campaign on both channels reaches each customer on whichever
  channels they allow; it never requires both.
- **Current state.** Permissions are read as they are now. Delivery must check them again
  just before sending (Phase 5).

### 4. One service: estimation and dispatch share the same SQL

```go
type Query struct {
	Segment  campaign.Segment
	Channels []customer.Channel // the campaign's channels: at least one, no repeats
	AsOf     time.Time          // where relative windows end
}
func (s *Service) CountAudience(ctx context.Context, q Query) (Audience, error)  // Matched, Reachable, ByChannel
func (s *Service) Recipients(ctx context.Context, q Query, after int64, limit int) ([]Recipient, error)
func (s *Service) CheckReferences(ctx context.Context, seg campaign.Segment) ([]campaign.Problem, error)
```

- **`CountAudience`** runs one query with `count(*) FILTER (WHERE …)` per channel.
- **`Recipients`** is "GetRecipients" under Go's naming conventions.
  - **Paging.** Keyset pagination (`c.id > after ORDER BY c.id LIMIT n`, n ≤ 1,000) over the
    primary key, so memory stays bounded by one batch.
  - **Contents.** Each recipient carries a name, city and language, plus contact details only
    for the channels they're eligible on.
  - **Logging.** Email and phone use the customer types, which mask themselves in logs.
    `Recipient` logs only its ID and channels.
- **Same audience logic.** Both methods use the same compiled WHERE clause and eligibility
  expressions.
- **Validation on every call.** Each call validates the segment's structure (exported as
  `campaign.Segment.Validate`), then the query, then references. Failures come back as
  `*campaign.ValidationError`.

### 5. References and money

- **References.** Every city, category and country code must exist. Unknown values are
  reported with their field path and the new code `unknown`, matched exactly, case included.
  It takes one query per reference table.
- **Money.** `min_try` and `max_try` are whole lira; `commerce.Money` and the database use
  kuruş, so the compiler multiplies by 100. Bounds are inclusive. Campaign validation caps
  both at 1,000,000,000 TRY, so the conversion can't overflow.

## Index review

The existing indexes match the compiled per-customer probes:

- `orders (customer_id, ordered_at)`;
- the primary keys of `basket_items`, `notification_permissions`, `order_items` and
  `products`;
- `customers (city_id)`.

I'll run EXPLAIN ANALYZE on the seeded data, then record the results and the candidates that
remain in DECISIONS.md:

- a partial index for completed orders;
- a partial index for granted permissions;
- a covering index on `order_items`.

## Implementation

```text
internal/segmentation/
  compile.go         pure compiler: Segment + AsOf → WHERE clause and arguments; eligibility per channel
  references.go      collect names with their field paths; check them against the reference tables
  segmentation.go    Service, Query, Audience, Recipient; CountAudience and Recipients through GORM Raw
internal/campaign/
  segment.go         export Segment.Validate(); cap min_try and max_try
  problems.go        add ProblemUnknown
  service.go         referenceChecker interface; Create and Update add unknown references to the 422 problems
cmd/api/main.go     segmentation.NewService(db) passed to campaign.NewService
```

## Tests

- **Unit, no database:**
  - the compiler's SQL text and arguments for each condition;
  - nesting, negation and the empty root;
  - windows, and conditions without one;
  - the kuruş conversion;
  - query validation;
  - campaign tests with a fake reference checker, including a 422 for an unknown city.
- **Integration, with a hand-made fixture** where each case has a known answer:
  - **Same-row filters:** a basket line and a purchase must each satisfy both their window and
    their categories.
  - **Order status:** cancelled and refunded orders are ignored.
  - **Ranges:** `order_count` and `total_spent` boundaries, e.g. 999.90 vs 1,000 TRY.
  - **Windows:** they end at `AsOf`.
  - **Logic:** negation and nested all/any groups.
  - **Eligibility:**
    - a customer without a phone;
    - a revoked SMS permission;
    - email-only permission in a two-channel campaign;
    - an empty segment.
  - **`Recipients` batches:** they cover every eligible customer exactly once.
  - **References:** unknown ones are reported, and an injection-looking name is treated as
    data.
- **Against the seeded dataset:** the Phase 2A example's SMS-eligible count must equal the
  seeder's in-memory analysis (56).

## Docs

- **DECISIONS.md:** entries 22 (segmentation) and 23 (the index review).
- **README.md:** status, layout and further improvements.
- **PHASE_REVIEWS.md:** a Phase 4 entry.

1. **Checks:** `make check` and `make test-integration`.
2. **Query plans:** EXPLAIN ANALYZE on the seeded data for the example segment and a complex
   nested one, both for a count and for one batch of recipients.

```
// bounds checks a range: at least one end, each from 0 to limit, and lo not above hi. It
// reports every problem; the order of the ends is only checked when both are valid on their own.
func (v *segmentValidator) bounds(path, loName, hiName string, lo, hi *int, limit int) {
	if lo == nil && hi == nil {
		v.ps.addDetail(path, ProblemRequired, loName+" or "+hiName)
		return
	}
	inRange := func(p *int) bool { return p == nil || (*p >= 0 && *p <= limit) }
	loValid, hiValid := inRange(lo), inRange(hi)
```

// checkReferences reports unknown countries, cities and categories as a *ValidationError, like
// any other problem with a draft.
func (s *Service) checkReferences(ctx context.Context, seg Segment) error {
	problems, err := s.references.CheckReferences(ctx, seg)
	if err != nil {
		return fmt.Errorf("check segment references: %w", err)
	}
	if len(problems) > 0 {
		return &ValidationError{Problems: problems}
	}
	return nil
}

// Grant records, as of at, that the customer allows marketing messages on ch. It fails with
// ErrUnreachable when the customer has no contact point for ch.
func (c *Customer) Grant(ch Channel, at time.Time) error {
	if err := c.checkPermissionChange(ch, at); err != nil {
		return err
	}
	if !c.reachableOn(ch) {
		return fmt.Errorf("grant %s permission: %w", ch, ErrUnreachable)
	}
	c.permissions[ch] = Permission{Channel: ch, Granted: true, UpdatedAt: at}
	return nil
}

// ParseEmail validates s as a single email address and returns it trimmed and lowercased.
// The error never contains s.
func ParseEmail(s string) (Email, error) {
	address := strings.ToLower(strings.TrimSpace(s))
	parsed, err := mail.ParseAddress(address)
	if err != nil || parsed.Name != "" || parsed.Address != address {
		return Email{}, fmt.Errorf("%w email address", ErrInvalid)
	}
	return Email{address: address}, nil
}

// seededDatabase returns a database holding the default seed, generated as of asOf. It gathers
// the statistics autovacuum would, so query plans are realistic from the start and don't
// change while a benchmark runs.
func seededDatabase(t testing.TB) *gorm.DB {
	t.Helper()
	db := databasetest.New(t)
	if _, err := seed.Run(t.Context(), db, seed.DefaultOptions(), asOf); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("seed: %v", err)
	}
	if err := db.WithContext(t.Context()).Exec("ANALYZE").Error; err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("analyze: %v", err)
	}
… [13 more characters]
```

**Edit** `<repo>/internal/segmentation/compile.go`

```
// countSQL counts the customers the segment matches, those who can receive any of the
// channels, and those who can receive each one, in a single pass. MATERIALIZED keeps
// PostgreSQL from inlining the audience into each count, which would test every permission
// twice; on the seeded data it makes counting every customer about 7 times faster.
func countSQL(segment expr, flags []eligibility) expr {
	var selects, counts, columns []string
	var args []any
	for _, f := range flags {
		selects = append(selects, f.test.sql+" AS "+f.column)
		args = append(args, f.test.args...)
		counts = append(counts, "count(*) FILTER (WHERE "+f.column+") AS "+f.column)
		columns = append(columns, f.column)
… [356 more characters]
```

// Create validates d, including the references in its segment, and saves it as a new draft.
func (s *Service) Create(ctx context.Context, d Draft) (*Campaign, error) {
	c, err := New(d, s.time())
	if err = s.addUnknownReferences(ctx, d.Segment, err); err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	if err := s.repo.Create(ctx, c); err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	return c, nil
}

// addUnknownReferences adds the unknown countries, cities and categories in seg to err, the
// outcome of validating a draft, so a draft's problems are all reported at once. It checks
// references only in a segment that is valid on its own, and only when err is nil or a
// *ValidationError; any other error, such as ErrNotDraft, is returned as it is.
func (s *Service) addUnknownReferences(ctx context.Context, seg Segment, err error) error {
	var invalid *ValidationError
	if err != nil && !errors.As(err, &invalid) || seg.Validate() != nil {
		return err
	}
	unknown, checkErr := s.references.CheckReferences(ctx, seg)
	if checkErr != nil {
		return checkErr // the checker describes what it was doing
… [202 more characters]

**Context.** Segments are untrusted input, from marketers now and from an LLM later. Two

> **Status — Phase 4 (segmentation engine).** On top of the Phase 1 foundation (PostgreSQL,
> migrations, health probes, structured logs, graceful shutdown, and a web app showing the
> API's readiness), the customer and commerce model holds 5,000 synthetic customers and their
> shopping activity. Marketers can manage campaign drafts through the REST API. The
> segmentation engine compiles a draft's segment into parameterised SQL and counts or lists
> the customers it reaches on each channel. Saving a draft checks that the cities, categories
> and countries it names exist. AI, sending and scheduling arrive in later phases.

Phase 4 is implemented and verified, and nothing is committed yet. I've proposed four commits below for your approval.

#### What was built
- **`internal/segmentation`** turns a segment into a single parameterised PostgreSQL query. Segment values only ever travel as bind arguments, never as SQL text, so LLM output can't inject SQL.
- **`CountAudience`** returns how many customers a segment matches, how many can be reached at all, and how many per channel.
- **`Recipients`** returns batches of up to 1,000 customers. Each one carries contact details only for the channels they have granted. A customer allowing only email is reached by email in a two-channel campaign.
- **Shared logic:** estimates and dispatch use the same validation and the same query. On the seeded data, paging through every batch gives exactly the counted totals.
- **Draft saves** now report every unknown city, category or country with its field path. These come back together with the draft's other problems, in one 422.

#### Verification
- `make check` passes (lint: 0 issues), and so does `make test-integration`.
- **Test fixture:** six hand-made customers whose expected results are known. They cover every condition type, cancelled and refunded orders, the 999.90 vs 1,000 TRY boundary, and each eligibility case. An injection-shaped city name is treated as plain data.
- **Seeded data:** the Phase 2A example reaches 56 customers by SMS, the same as the seed's own analysis.
- **Mutation check:** I deliberately introduced six bugs into the compiler. Each one made its intended test fail.
- **End to end through nginx:** unknown names get a 422 listing each one, and known names get a 201. I deleted the test drafts afterwards; the dev database still has its 5,000 customers and no campaigns.

#### Two changes beyond the plan
- **Faster counts.** Measuring showed PostgreSQL checking each permission twice. Wrapping the count in a materialised CTE made counting every customer 7× faster (17 → 2.4 ms). No indexes were added (decision C). The slowest measured call is the nested segment's count at 45 ms. The index candidates are recorded in DECISIONS.md §23 and the README's further improvements.
- **Unknown names reported in the same 422.** My first version only reported them once the rest of the draft was valid. Since your plan said "add unknown references to the 422 problems", I fixed it during my own review.

#### Worth a closer look
- **Phase 5 must re-check permissions** for each recipient just before sending. Everything except the time windows is read live.
- **City names are matched across all countries.** If a name exists in two countries, pairing the `city` condition with a `country` condition disambiguates it.
- **Name matching is exact:** "Istanbul" is reported as `unknown`. Suggesting the correct spelling is on the further-improvements list.

#### Proposed commits
I checked that each commit builds and passes its tests on its own:

| # | Message | Files |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `feat(campaign): export segment validation and bound numeric conditions` | `internal/campaign/segment.go`, `segment_test.go`, `problems.go` |
| 2 | `feat(segmentation): compile segments to SQL and count or list their audience` | `internal/segmentation/*` (5 files) |
| 3 | `feat(campaign): reject drafts that name unknown cities, categories or countries` | `internal/campaign/service.go`, `http_test.go`, `cmd/api/main.go` |
| 4 | `docs: record phase 4 segmentation decisions and review` | `DECISIONS.md`, `README.md`, `PHASE_REVIEWS.md` |

// bounds checks a "min"/"max" range: at least one end, neither negative, and min not above
// max. It reports every problem; the order of the ends is only checked when both are valid on
// their own.
func (v *segmentValidator) bounds(path string, lo, hi *int) {
	if lo == nil && hi == nil {
		v.ps.addDetail(path, ProblemRequired, "min or max")
		return
	}
	loValid, hiValid := lo == nil || *lo >= 0, hi == nil || *hi >= 0
	if !loValid {
		v.ps.add(path+".min", ProblemInvalid)
	}

**Edit** `<repo>/internal/segmentation/compile.go`

```
// between compares a scalar subquery with inclusive bounds, at least one of which is set.
func between(subquery string, args []any, lo, hi *int) expr {
	switch {
	case lo != nil && hi != nil:
		return expr{subquery + " BETWEEN ? AND ?", append(args, *lo, *hi)}
	case lo != nil:
		return expr{subquery + " >= ?", append(args, *lo)}
	default:
		return expr{subquery + " <= ?", append(args, *hi)}
	}
}
```

**Edit** `<repo>/DECISIONS.md`

```
- **One pair of bounds, money in kuruş.**
  - `order_count` and `total_spent` both take `min` and `max`, inclusive, neither negative,
    and `min` not above `max`.
  - For `total_spent` they are kuruş, the unit of `commerce.Money` and of the prices paid, so
    the compiler compares them with the sum as stored, without conversion: `"min": 100000` is
    1,000 TRY.
  - The DSL's earlier `min_try`/`max_try` in whole lira are gone.
```

  in the tool's schema and examples: a model that writes lira targets spends 100 times
  smaller.
- **DSL version 1 changed in place.** No stored or released campaign used `min_try` or
  `max_try`, and the API now rejects them as unknown fields.

I removed `MinTRY` and `MaxTRY`. `order_count` and `total_spent` now share `Min`/`Max`, and the compiler compares spend bounds with the order totals as stored, without converting.

**What it means for spend amounts:** since nothing converts them any more, `total_spent` bounds are in kuruş, the unit the order tables already use. 1,000 TRY is written `"min": 100000`.

**One change you didn't ask for:** I also dropped the caps I'd added earlier in this phase (1,000,000,000 TRY and 1,000,000 orders). They only existed to stop the ×100 conversion from overflowing. Bounds now follow Phase 3's rule again: neither negative, and `min` not above `max`.

**Risk for the AI phase:** a model that writes amounts in lira would target spends 100 times smaller. The AI tool's schema and examples will need to say amounts are in kuruş. I've noted this in DECISIONS.md §22 and the phase review.

**Verification:**
- `make check` and `make test-integration` pass.
- I rebuilt the stack and tested through nginx:
  - a draft with kuruş `min`/`max` saves (201);
  - `min_try` is now rejected as an unknown field (400 `malformed_json`);
  - `min` above `max` gets a 422 on `max`.
- I deleted the test draft afterwards.

**Docs:** DECISIONS.md §20 now carries an amendment note instead of losing the old wording. §22, the README and the Phase 4 review are updated too.

#### Proposed commits
Commit 1 now also includes `store_test.go`, which uses the new fields. I re-checked that each commit builds and passes its tests on its own.

| # | Message | Files |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `feat(campaign)!: export segment validation; total_spent takes min and max in kuruş` | `internal/campaign/segment.go`, `segment_test.go`, `problems.go`, `store_test.go` |
| 2 | `feat(segmentation): compile segments to SQL and count or list their audience` | `internal/segmentation/*` |
| 3 | `feat(campaign): reject drafts that name unknown cities, categories or countries` | `internal/campaign/service.go`, `http_test.go`, `cmd/api/main.go` |
| 4 | `docs: record phase 4 segmentation decisions and review` | `DECISIONS.md`, `README.md`, `PHASE_REVIEWS.md` |

The `!` on commit 1 marks a breaking change: the Phase 3 API accepted `min_try` and now rejects it. Its message would say to send `min`/`max` in kuruş. Nothing is released yet, so drop the `!` if you'd rather not flag it.

```

// addUnknownReferences adds the unknown countries, cities and categories in seg to err, the
// outcome of validating a draft, so a draft's problems are all reported at once. An error
// that isn't a *ValidationError, such as ErrNotDraft, is returned as it is.
func (s *Service) addUnknownReferences(ctx context.Context, seg Segment, err error) error {
	var invalid *ValidationError
	if err != nil && !errors.As(err, &invalid) {
		return err
	}
	unknown, checkErr := s.references.CheckReferences(ctx, seg)
	if checkErr != nil {

// Package segmentation evaluates campaign segments. It checks that the countries, cities and
// categories a segment names exist, compiles the segment into parameterised SQL over the
// customer and commerce tables, and counts or lists the customers each channel can reach.
//
// Audience estimates and dispatch use the same compiled query, so they apply the same rules.
// The AI agent's tools and the delivery phase call the service directly; there is no REST API.

**Edit** `<repo>/DECISIONS.md`

```
  - Formats are the domain's job (decision 17), so only presence is tested. A granted SMS
    permission without a phone, which the domain refuses but the table can hold, is not
    trusted.
  - One fixed SQL fragment selects both channels' eligibility; the campaign's channels are
    bind parameters, so the query text never depends on them.
```

**Edit** `<repo>/DECISIONS.md`

```
  - Every country code, city and category a segment names must exist, matched exactly (case
    included). Unknown ones are problems with code `unknown` at their field path.
  - `campaign` owns the DSL, so it lists the names with their field paths
    (`Segment.References`). The segmentation service looks them all up in one query.
  - Campaign drafts are checked on create and update through a `referenceChecker` interface
    that `campaign` declares and the segmentation service implements (wired in `cmd/api`), so
    `campaign` doesn't import the engine.
  - Unknown references are reported with the draft's other problems. A segment with problems
    of its own gets no reference report, so a malformed name is never also called unknown.
```

- **Saving a draft queries the database:** one reference lookup, when the segment names
  anything.

I simplified `internal/campaign` and `internal/segmentation`. Behaviour is unchanged, all checks pass, and nothing is committed yet. I left the HTTP handlers and the store alone: their helpers are each reused, so rewriting them would have been churn.

#### Rules that were defined twice
- **Condition parameters:** each condition type now lists the parameters it takes right beside their checks. `allowedParams` repeated that list and is gone.
- **Which DSL fields hold names, and their field paths:** this was coded in both packages. It now lives only in `campaign`, as `Segment.References()`, so segmentation needs no knowledge of the DSL's shape.
- **"Don't check names in a malformed segment":** this decision was made in two places and now lives only in `CheckReferences`. The campaign service just adds the unknown names to the draft's other problems.

#### Fewer helpers and less boilerplate
- **Channel eligibility:** a per-channel loop built the count and recipient queries, then the scanning code hard-coded the two channels anyway. It's now one fixed SQL fragment, with the campaign's channels passed as parameters. Each query sits next to the code that reads its results.
- **Compiler:** down from 13 functions to 7. Every condition's SQL is in one switch, and basket lines and order lines share one category filter.
- **Reference check:** down from 4 functions to 1, and one query instead of one per kind of name.
- **Smaller pieces:** `Segment.Problems()` replaces `Validate()`, so callers no longer unwrap an error to get the list. One generic `clonePtr` replaces the hand-written copying, and two single-use helpers were folded in.

The seven production files went from 1,230 to 1,122 lines.

#### What changed visibly
- Only the generated SQL text: a negated group now reads `NOT ((…))`, and categories are matched through a products subquery.
- I benchmarked the new query shapes against the old ones, and the difference was within run-to-run noise. The recorded numbers are refreshed: the nested segment's count is now 53 ms (median of five runs), against 45 ms this morning. The A/B puts that down to the machine, not the code.
- One test moved from campaign to segmentation, following the rule it tests.

#### Verification
- `make check` and `make test-integration` pass.
- I deliberately introduced eight bugs, including two new ones for the rewritten parts. Each made its intended test fail.
- Through nginx: unknown names are reported together with a too-long SMS, and a padded name is reported only as `invalid`. I deleted the test draft afterwards.
- Segmentation coverage without a database fell from 60 % to 55 %, because the audience queries now only run against PostgreSQL. With PostgreSQL it's 93 %.
- DECISIONS.md and the Phase 4 review are updated.

#### Proposed commits
Commit 1 now also includes `campaign.go` and `content.go`, because of `clonePtr`. I checked that each commit builds and passes its tests on its own.

## 7. Observability: structured logs, request IDs, health probes

**Decision.** `log/slog` writes JSON (text locally). Every request produces one access-log line
with `request_id`, `method`, `path`, `status`, `bytes` and `duration_ms`; 5xx responses log at
error level and successful health probes at debug level, so the 10-second healthcheck does
not drown real traffic. nginx sets `X-Request-Id`, which chi adopts, so web and API logs
correlate. `GET /healthz` (liveness) checks no dependencies, so a database outage never
restarts a healthy process; `GET /readyz` (readiness) pings PostgreSQL within 2 s and answers
503 on failure, logging the details instead of returning them.

**Consequences.** Outages show up as "unhealthy", not as restart loops; probe responses
leak no internals.

Here's the proposal. Nothing is built: decision 19 rules out endpoints that send or schedule before delivery exists, so these would ship with the delivery phase once you approve them.

#### Endpoints

Everything lives under the existing `/api/campaigns` and uses its JSON error format.

| Need | Request | Success | Errors |
|---|---|---|---|
| Send now | `POST /api/campaigns/{id}/send` | `202`, the campaign in `sending`; `Location: /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery` | 404; 409 `already_dispatched`; 412 `campaign_changed`; 422 `invalid_campaign` |
| Schedule or move | `PUT /api/campaigns/{id}/schedule` with `{"send_at": "2026-11-20T09:00:00+03:00"}` | `200`, the campaign in `scheduled` | 404; 409 `already_dispatched`; 412; 422 `invalid_campaign` (e.g. `send_at` in the past) |
| Unschedule | `DELETE /api/campaigns/{id}/schedule` | `200`, the campaign back in `draft` | 404; 409 `not_scheduled` |
| Dispatch status, progress, statistics | `GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery` | `200`, a snapshot (below) | 404 |
| Live progress | `GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery/events` | Server-sent events (SSE): the same snapshot, pushed each time it changes | 404 |

Dispatch status needs no endpoint of its own. The campaign already has a `status`; it gains `send_at`, `started_at` and `finished_at`, and the lifecycle gains `sent` (`sending → sent`).

#### The snapshot

```json
{
  "status": "sending",
  "send_at": null,
  "started_at": "2026-11-14T12:00:00Z",
  "finished_at": null,
  "recipients": 109,
  "messages": {"total": 135, "pending": 30, "sent": 60, "delivered": 38, "failed": 5, "skipped": 2},
  "by_channel": {
    "email": {"total": 79, "pending": 18, "sent": 35, "delivered": 22, "failed": 3, "skipped": 1},
    "sms":   {"total": 56, "pending": 12, "sent": 25, "delivered": 16, "failed": 2, "skipped": 1}
  }
}
```

The example uses the seeded Phase 2A audience sent on both channels: 109 recipients, 79 by email and 56 by SMS.

- **Message statuses:**
  - `pending`: queued;
  - `sent`: accepted by the provider;
  - `delivered`: a delivery receipt arrived;
  - `failed`;
  - `skipped`: the permission was revoked or the contact detail is missing at send time.

  Progress is `(total − pending) / total`.
- **Where the numbers come from:** the dispatch writes one row per recipient and channel. Those rows carry the per-recipient idempotency key that decision 11 requires. The totals are then fixed when sending starts, and the snapshot is a single grouped count over them.
- **Before sending:** a draft or scheduled campaign returns the same shape with zeros, so the UI needs no special case.

#### Rules

- **Each command is a lifecycle transition.** The update only applies while the status allows it, as Phase 3's writes already do. A double click, or two people at once, can't dispatch twice: the second request gets 409.
- **Send now** works on a draft or a scheduled campaign. **Schedule** works on a draft, or on a scheduled campaign to move its time.
- **`send_at`** is an RFC 3339 time with an offset, stored in UTC. It must be in the future and at most a year ahead.
- **A scheduled campaign is evaluated when it's sent:** its time windows end then, and its names are checked again then. If the segment no longer validates at that point (say a city was removed), it can't be dispatched. I'd mark it `failed` with the problems; the delivery phase settles that status.
- **The SSE stream** re-reads the snapshot every second, sends it when it changed, and sends a heartbeat every 15 s. It ends after `sent`.
  - It extends its own write deadline, since the server has a 30 s `WriteTimeout`.
  - It stops on shutdown (decision 8).
  - It sets `X-Accel-Buffering: no`, so nginx streams it without a config change.

  Polling `GET …/delivery` remains the fallback.

#### Decisions for you

- **A. Command style.**
  - (a) Action endpoints as above *(recommended)*: one per button, and the intent is explicit.
  - (b) `PATCH /api/campaigns/{id}` with a new status. This hides the side effects, and Phase 3 deliberately rejects a status sent by the client.
  - (c) A `dispatches` resource: an extra entity for something a campaign does once.
- **B. Send exactly what was reviewed.**
  - (a) Send and schedule require an `If-Match` header with the version tag (ETag) that `GET` returns. If the draft was edited since, they get 412 `campaign_changed`; without the header, 428 *(recommended: decision 19 deferred this lock until approval exists)*.
  - (b) No check: the last write wins.
- **C. Real time.**
  - (a) SSE plus the polling endpoint *(recommended)*. This settles the open "SSE vs WebSockets" question. Each stream reads the database itself, so several API instances work without PostgreSQL notifications.
  - (b) Polling only.
- **D. Statistics scope.**
  - (a) Per campaign, as above *(recommended)*.
  - (b) Also `GET /api/delivery/stats?from=&to=` across campaigns, for a dashboard.

#### Not included (say if you want them)

- `GET /api/campaigns/{id}/audience`: the audience estimate a reviewer would want to see before approving. The AI agent already gets it as a tool.
- Cancelling a dispatch in progress, or listing individual messages such as the failed ones.
- A `/metrics` endpoint. For now, each dispatch would log its totals and duration, as decision 7's structured logs do.
- Authentication. The API has none, so anyone who can reach it could send a campaign.

Approve or adjust these; for example "A: (a), B: (a), C: (a), D: (a)". I'll then plan the delivery phase around them: the queue choice left open in decision 11, mock providers and workers.

The plan is written. Over to you for review:

**Plan presented for approval:**

### Phase 5: Delivery — dispatch API, River queue, mock providers, live progress

#### Context

Approved campaigns must be sent asynchronously to the synthetic customers through mock
providers, with delivery tracked in real time. Phases 3 and 4 left the pieces this needs:

- a campaign lifecycle (`draft`, `scheduled`, `sending`);
- `segmentation.Recipients` with keyset batches;
- the rule that permissions are re-checked just before sending (DECISIONS §15, §22);
- no endpoint that sends anything yet (§19).

**Decisions taken:**

- **Dispatch endpoint.** A single `POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch`; without
  `scheduled_at`, the campaign is sent now.
- **Approval check.** No check that the dispatched version is the reviewed one for now; it is
  recorded in the README's further improvements.
- **Progress.** SSE plus a polling snapshot, with statistics per campaign.
- **Queue.** River with native scheduled jobs; no cron that polls for due campaigns.
- **Concurrency.** Atomic, conditional state transitions; repeated or concurrent dispatches
  get 409; idempotency is enforced by database constraints.
- **Processes.** A separate worker service; in-process mock providers; delayed delivery
  receipts.
- **Out of scope.** Authentication and authorization.

River v0.51.2 was checked in the module cache:

- `riverdatabasesql.New(*sql.DB)` works jobs in poll-only mode on GORM's existing pool. The
  producer re-fetches as soon as worker slots free up, so polling adds at most a poll
  interval of idle latency and doesn't cap throughput.
- `InsertTx` accepts GORM's `*sql.Tx`; GORM doesn't use prepared-statement mode.
- `InsertOpts.ScheduledAt` provides native scheduling.
- `UniqueOpts{ByArgs}` is backed by a unique index, but only while the job row exists:
  completed jobs are cleaned after 24 hours. Our own constraints must be the lasting guard.
- The client never reads `river_migration` at runtime, and River's migration 005 tolerates
  that table's absence.

#### Lifecycle (`internal/campaign`, domain)

- **Statuses.** `draft → scheduled → sending → sent`, plus `failed`.
- **Transitions** (`CanBecome`):
  - draft → scheduled or sending;
  - scheduled → sending or failed, and → draft (kept for a later unschedule endpoint);
  - sending → sent or failed.
- **New fields.** `scheduledAt`, `startedAt` and `finishedAt`, exposed in the campaign JSON
  as `scheduled_at`, `started_at` and `finished_at`. `updated_at` keeps meaning "content last
  changed".
- **Methods**, each guarded by `CanBecome`:
  - `Dispatch(scheduledAt *time.Time, now)`. It validates `scheduled_at` (after now, at most
    a year ahead; otherwise a `*ValidationError` on field `scheduled_at`) and answers
    `ErrNotDraft` for any non-draft.
  - `Start(now)`, `Finish(now)` and `Fail(now)`.
- **`failed`** happens only when a scheduled campaign's segment no longer validates as its
  dispatch starts, say a name it uses was removed. The job logs the problems.
- **Rendering.** `campaign.Fill(text, values)` replaces `{{ first_name }}`, `{{ last_name }}`
  and `{{ city }}`. Placeholders belong to the content rules in `content.go`.

#### API

| Request | Result |
|---|---|
| `POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch`, body optional: `{"scheduled_at": "2026-11-20T09:00:00+03:00"}` | `202`, the campaign (`sending` or `scheduled`), `Location: /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery`. 409 `already_dispatched` for any non-draft; 404; 422 `invalid_campaign` (bad `scheduled_at`, or names that no longer exist, re-checked through the existing `referenceChecker`); 400/413 as today |
| `GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery` | `200` snapshot: `status`, the three times, `recipients`, then `messages` and `by_channel` counts of `pending` / `sent` / `delivered` / `failed` / `skipped`, as proposed. A draft returns zeros |
| `GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery/events` | SSE: the same snapshot each time it changes |

#### Duplicate prevention: one layer per failure mode

1. **Conditional transition.** `UPDATE campaigns … WHERE id = ? AND status = 'draft'`.
   Concurrent or repeated requests get no row and answer 409.
2. **Same transaction.** The River dispatch job is inserted with `InsertTx` on that
   transaction, so the status change and the job commit together or not at all (no dual
   write).
3. **Unique dispatch job.** River's `UniqueOpts{ByArgs}` on `campaign_id` is a second guard.
4. **Unique delivery rows.** `deliveries UNIQUE (campaign_id, customer_id, channel)`:
   enumerating again inserts nothing twice (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`).
5. **Conditional message updates.** There is one send job per delivery, unique by delivery
   ID. Message changes only apply from the expected state (`WHERE status = 'pending'`, or
   `'sent'` for receipts).
6. **Provider idempotency key.** The delivery ID is the key, so a retry after a crash
   mid-send can't send twice.
7. **Consistency CHECKs.** `scheduled` ⇒ `scheduled_at`; `sending`/`sent` ⇒ `started_at`;
   `sent`/`failed` ⇒ `finished_at`.

#### Schema (goose)

- **`migrations/00005_river.sql`:** River's schema at v0.51.2, its migrations 002–008 with
  the `/* TEMPLATE: schema */` markers removed. 001, River's own migration-tracking table, is
  left out because goose tracks migrations. Down runs River's downs in reverse. The header
  records the River version and how to add the next River migration on upgrade.
- **`migrations/00006_delivery.sql`:**
  - **Campaigns.** The status CHECK gains `sent` and `failed`; the three time columns are
    added with the consistency CHECKs above.
  - **`deliveries`:**
    - `id`;
    - `campaign_id` and `customer_id`, with foreign keys;
    - `channel`, checked;
    - `status`, checked: `pending`, `sent`, `delivered`, `failed`, `skipped`;
    - `provider_message_id`;
    - `error`, a reason code, never personal data;
    - `created_at` and `updated_at`.

    It has `UNIQUE (campaign_id, customer_id, channel)` and an index on
    `(campaign_id, status, channel)` for snapshots and the "anything pending?" check.

#### Jobs (`internal/delivery`, run by `cmd/worker`)

- **`dispatch`** (`campaign_id`; unique; `ScheduledAt` for scheduled campaigns):
  1. `Start` the campaign: scheduled → sending, recording `started_at`. A campaign already
     `sending` continues, since this is a retry; any other status means nothing to do.
  2. Enumerate the audience with `segmentation.Recipients`, in batches of 1,000.
     - `AsOf` is `started_at`, so a retry sees the same windows.
     - It resumes after the last customer already enumerated.
     - It inserts one `pending` delivery per recipient and channel.
  3. Enqueue one `send` job per pending delivery, in chunks.
  4. Finish at once if nothing is pending (an empty audience).

  Because every row exists before any send runs, "no pending delivery" reliably means done.
  An invalid segment calls `Fail` and cancels the job; any other error lets River retry.
- **`send`** (`delivery_id`; unique). It loads the delivery, the customer's contact details,
  names and city, the current permission and the campaign's content.
  - **Not pending:** done.
  - **Permission revoked or contact detail gone:** `skipped`.
  - **Rendered SMS over 160 characters:** `failed`, reason `too_long` (decision 21).
  - **Provider accepts:** `sent`, with the provider's ID.
  - **Permanent rejection:** `failed`.
  - **Transient error:** River retries; on the last attempt, `failed`, reason
    `provider_unavailable`.

  It then finishes the campaign if nothing is pending.
- **`receipt`** (`delivery_id`, outcome; `ScheduledAt` = the provider's delay): `sent` →
  `delivered` or `failed`. It retries if the message isn't `sent` yet.
- **Finishing a campaign.** The finishing transition (sending → sent) is conditional and
  runs after each send or skip commits. Whichever job commits last always sees nothing
  pending, so no campaign gets stuck; a second, concurrent finish does nothing.
- **Mock providers.** In process, behind a `provider` interface the delivery package declares:
  - latency of 20–200 ms;
  - about 3 % transient errors and 1 % permanent rejections;
  - deduplication by idempotency key;
  - a receipt scheduled through the queue 1–5 s later: about 95 % delivered.

  The rates are code constants, and tests inject deterministic fakes.

#### Live progress

- **Snapshot.** The campaign through `campaign.Service.Get`, plus one grouped count over
  `deliveries` and a count of distinct recipients.
- **SSE stream.**
  - It recomputes every second, sends when the snapshot changed, and sends `: ping` every
    15 s.
  - It extends its write deadline before each write, since the server's `WriteTimeout` is
    30 s.
  - It stops when the client leaves, on shutdown (cmd/api passes its signal context, per
    decision 8), or after the final snapshot: the campaign is `sent` or `failed` and nothing
    is pending or awaiting a receipt.
  - It sets `X-Accel-Buffering: no` for nginx.
- **Routing.** `cmd/api` mounts the delivery routes on `/api/campaigns/{id}/delivery` beside
  the campaign handler. Chi routing for this has to be checked first; the fallback is the
  campaign router mounting a handler it is given.

#### Processes and layout

- **`cmd/api`:** an insert-only River client (`riverdatabasesql.New` on GORM's pool) wrapped
  as `delivery.Queue`. It is passed to `campaign.Service` as a consumer-declared
  `dispatcher`, which `Store.Dispatch` calls inside its transaction.
- **`cmd/worker` (new):**
  - configuration, database, and the segmentation and campaign services;
  - a River client with the three workers (poll-only, 50 workers, fetch poll 200 ms);
  - the mock providers;
  - graceful stop on SIGTERM.

  It doesn't migrate: Compose starts it after `seed`.
- **`compose.yaml`:** a `worker` service from the same image (`/app/worker`), after
  postgres is healthy and seed has completed, with `stop_grace_period: 15s`.
- **Makefile:** `make worker`. No new configuration variables.
- **`internal/delivery`:**
  - `delivery.go`: package doc, `Queue`, job arguments;
  - `jobs.go`: dispatch, send and receipt workers, finishing;
  - `store.go`: deliveries, with conditional updates;
  - `mock.go`;
  - `http.go`: snapshot and SSE.
- **`internal/campaign`:** `campaign.go`, `service.go` (`Dispatch`, `Start`, `Finish`,
  `Fail`), `store.go`, `http.go` and `content.go` (`Fill`).
- **Dependencies:** `github.com/riverqueue/river` and its `riverdatabasesql` driver at
  v0.51.2.

#### Tests

- **Unit:**
  - lifecycle transitions and `scheduled_at` rules;
  - `Fill`;
  - the dispatch handler: 202 now or scheduled, 409, 404, 422, empty and malformed bodies;
  - send-job decisions with fakes: skipped, too long, retry, last attempt, permanent
    failure;
  - mock provider idempotency.
- **Integration (PostgreSQL, `rivertest`):**
  - **Concurrency.** 20 concurrent dispatches give one 202, nineteen 409s and exactly one
    dispatch job. A repeat gets 409. An enqueue failure rolls the status back.
  - **Scheduling.** A scheduled dispatch inserts the job with that `ScheduledAt`.
  - **Dispatch on the Phase 4 fixture.** The deliveries match `Recipients` per channel.
    Running it twice adds no rows and no send jobs.
  - **A full run with real River workers.** The campaign reaches `sent`. A permission
    revoked after dispatch gives `skipped`. The counts are consistent.
  - **Seeded data.** The Phase 2A example on both channels gives totals equal to
    `CountAudience` at dispatch.
  - **SSE.** It streams, sends heartbeats, and ends when final or cancelled.
  - **Schema.** The new constraints, and the migration round trip including River's.

#### Docs

- **DECISIONS.md:**
  - **24, River.** Schema through goose, the poll-only driver on the GORM pool,
    transactional insert, scheduled jobs, the separate worker.
  - **25, delivery model.** The idempotency layers, message statuses, finishing, the
    permission re-check, rendering.
  - **26, dispatch and progress API.**
  - **Open questions.** Answer queue, real time and mock provider.
- **README.md:**

  - further improvements: approving the reviewed version (If-Match, 412), unschedule and
    cancel, cross-campaign statistics, `/metrics`, real providers with webhooks, multi-part
    SMS, authentication.
- **CLAUDE.md:** add `make worker` to the commands table.
- **PHASE_REVIEWS.md:** the Phase 5 entry.

2. `docker compose up --build`, then through nginx:

   - watch `curl -N …/delivery/events` until `sent`;

   - schedule a dispatch two minutes ahead and see it start on time without a cron.
3. Delete the test campaigns afterwards; the customers still number 5,000.
