**Plan presented for approval:**

### Phase 5 (revised): Delivery — dispatch, River queues, mock providers, live progress

#### Context

Approved campaigns must be sent asynchronously through mock providers, with delivery tracked
in real time. Phase 4 left:

- a lifecycle (`draft`, `scheduled`, `sending`);
- keyset `segmentation.Recipients`;
- the rule that permissions are re-checked just before sending.

**Decided so far:**

- one `POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch` (no `scheduled_at` means now);
- no approval precondition yet, noted in the README;
- SSE plus a snapshot, with statistics per campaign;
- River with native scheduled jobs (no cron);
- conditional transitions, 409 when already dispatched, and idempotency through constraints;
- a separate worker, in-process mock providers and delayed receipts;
- no authentication.

**Facts checked in River v0.51.2:**

- `riverdatabasesql.New(*sql.DB)` works jobs in poll-only mode on GORM's pool.
- `InsertTx` accepts GORM's `*sql.Tx`.
- `QueueConfig.MaxWorkers` is set per queue.
- `rivermigrate` provides `Migrate` and `Validate`.
- Unique jobs only hold while the job row exists; completed jobs are cleaned after 24 h.
- `JobCancel` and `JobSnooze` exist.

#### 1. Processes, queues, concurrency and rate limits (points 1 and 5)

**One active worker.** `cmd/worker` takes a PostgreSQL session advisory lock before starting
River. A second replica blocks on that lock as a hot standby and takes over only when the
active one exits. So the system supports one active sender, plus optional standbys.

Several active workers would add no throughput, because the per-channel limit, not the worker,
is the bottleneck. A per-process limiter is therefore global, with no extra infrastructure.

**Queues.**

| Queue | Jobs | Concurrency (`MaxWorkers`) | Rate limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| `sms` | SMS sends | 20 | 100 provider requests/s |
| `email` | email sends | 20 | 100 provider requests/s |
| `default` | dispatch, receipts | 10 | none (no provider call) |

- **Concurrency and rate are separate settings.** Concurrency caps the provider calls in
  flight. The rate caps how many calls start per second.
- **The rate limit covers every provider call, retries included.** The limit is a
  `golang.org/x/time/rate` limiter per channel, at 100/s with a burst of 1: calls are spaced
  at least 10 ms apart, so no one-second window holds more than 100. The send worker waits on
  its channel's limiter immediately before the call.
- **Channels are independent.** Each channel has its own queue, workers and limiter, so a
  slow or saturated SMS provider can't hold email.
- **Settings.** Concurrency and rate live in one per-channel table of code constants.

**The API** holds an insert-only River client on the same pool.

#### 2. Transactions and locking (points 2 and 4)

Every transaction is short, and none is open during a provider call. A transaction that
touches a campaign and its deliveries always locks the campaign row first (`SELECT … FOR
UPDATE`), so they can't deadlock.

**Dispatch request (API), one transaction:**

1. lock the campaign;
2. apply the domain transition `Dispatch(scheduledAt, now)`: a draft becomes `sending` or
   `scheduled`, anything else is `ErrNotDraft`, answered as 409 `already_dispatched`;
3. save it with a conditional update;
4. `InsertTx` the `dispatch` job: unique by campaign, with `ScheduledAt` when scheduled.

Concurrent requests queue on the row lock; the first wins and the rest get 409.

**`dispatch` job, one transaction (point 4: no checkpoint, no extra table):**

1. Lock the campaign. `scheduled` becomes `Start` (`sending`, `started_at`). `sending`
   continues, since this is the immediate case or a retry. Any other status means nothing to
   do.
2. For each batch of 1,000 from `segmentation.Recipients` (with `AsOf = started_at`), in the
   same transaction:
   - insert one `pending` delivery per recipient and channel (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
     RETURNING id`);
   - `InsertManyTx` their send jobs into their channel's queue.
3. With no deliveries at all, `Finish`.

Commit makes every delivery and its send job visible together, or nothing. No send can run
before the audience is complete, so "nothing pending" can't come early. A failed transaction
rolls back whole and River retries it.

An invalid segment instead runs a short transaction that marks the campaign `failed`, and
the job ends with `JobCancel`. One transaction suits these audiences (5,000 customers, up to
10,000 rows and jobs, about a second). Millions would need batching with a checkpoint; that
is noted as a limit, not built.

**Completing a message, one short transaction, after the provider call:**

1. lock the campaign row;
2. `UPDATE deliveries SET status = … WHERE id = ? AND status = 'pending'`; no row means it
   was already completed, so stop;
3. if no delivery of the campaign is `pending`, apply `Finish` (sending → sent, conditional);
4. commit.

The campaign lock serialises completions of one campaign. Each one starts only after the
previous one committed, and reads with a fresh snapshot. So the last completion always sees
zero pending, and no campaign can be left in `sending`. At 100 messages/s per channel, the
serialised transactions of about 1 ms each are far from a bottleneck.

**Receipt:** `UPDATE … WHERE id = ? AND status = 'accepted'`, with no campaign lock: receipts
never change a campaign's status. If the message is still `pending`, the job snoozes
(`JobSnooze`).

#### 3. Lifecycle and statuses (point 7)

- **Campaigns:**
  - `draft → scheduled → sending → sent`, and `scheduled` or `sending` → `failed`;
  - `scheduled → draft` stays in `CanBecome` for a later unschedule endpoint;
  - new times `scheduled_at`, `started_at` and `finished_at`.
- **Messages:**
  - `pending` → `accepted` (the provider took the request);
  - `accepted` → `delivered`, or `failed` with reason `undelivered`, from the receipt;
  - `pending` → `failed` (`rejected`, `too_long`, `provider_unavailable`) or `skipped`
    (permission revoked or contact detail gone).

  This renames the proposal's `sent` to `accepted`, so that provider acceptance is never
  read as delivery.
- **When a campaign is `sent`:** when no message is `pending`, meaning every message was
  accepted, failed or skipped. It doesn't wait for receipts.
- **Receipts arrive after `sent`.** They only move messages from `accepted` to `delivered`
  or `failed`. The snapshot keeps changing, and the campaign stays `sent`.
- **Snapshot:**
  - `status` and the three times;
  - `recipients`;
  - `messages` and `by_channel`, counting `pending`, `accepted`, `delivered`, `failed` and
    `skipped`;
  - `final`: true when the campaign is `sent` or `failed` and nothing is `pending` or
    `accepted`.
- **SSE ends exactly when `final` becomes true.** It sends `event: done` with the final
  snapshot and closes; the client closes its EventSource on `done`, since it would otherwise
  reconnect. A draft gets one snapshot and `done`. Receipts that never arrive from a real
  provider would need a receipt timeout, which is a further improvement.

#### 4. Provider idempotency and the deterministic mock (points 3 and 6)

**Interface.** The `provider` interface is declared by `internal/delivery`:
`Send(ctx, message) (providerID string, err error)`. The message carries an idempotency key,
`delivery-<id>`. Errors are permanent (a rejection) or transient (anything else, timeouts
included).

**Durable idempotency.** The mock keeps its own state in a `mock_provider_messages` table
keyed by idempotency key: provider ID, outcome, attempts, accepted time. That table stands in
for a real provider's database.

- A repeated call with the same key returns the stored result, and never schedules a second
  receipt, across restarts too.
- On first acceptance, the mock's own transaction stores the result and inserts the `receipt`
  job, unique by delivery. That job stands in for the provider's webhook.

**Determinism.** The outcome is a pure function of the key: FNV-1a of the key, modulo 100.

| Bucket | Behaviour |
|---|---|
| 0 | Permanent rejection, so `failed` |
| 1 | Accepts, then answers after the 2 s call deadline on the first attempt. The retry returns the stored acceptance (the idempotency demonstration) |
| 2–3 | Transient error on the first attempt, accepted on the retry |
| 4–5 | Accepted; the receipt says undelivered |
| others | Accepted; the receipt says delivered |

Latency and receipt delay are fixed per channel: 50 ms and 2 s for SMS, 80 ms and 3 s for
email. The bucket function can be swapped, and worker tests use scripted fakes instead of
the mock.

**Limits of exactly-once (to record in DECISIONS):**

- Messages are sent at least once. Retries, crashes between the provider's acceptance and
  our commit, and timeouts can all repeat a call, and the stable idempotency key makes those
  repeats harmless only if the provider honours it.
- Real providers keep keys for a limited window, typically about 24 hours.
- Without key support, a duplicate is possible exactly in that crash gap.
- Carriers can still duplicate SMS, and receipts can be late, missing or wrong. So
  `delivered` is best effort.

#### 5. River migrations (point 8)

| | goose (River's SQL copied into migrations) | River's native migrator (`rivermigrate`) |
|---|---|---|
| History | One goose history | Our tables in goose; River's tracked in `river_migration` |
| Upgrade | Generate River's new SQL into a new goose file by hand; easy to forget | Bump the dependency; the next start applies River's migrations |
| Correctness | Our copy can drift from River's tested SQL | River's own tested, versioned SQL |
| Convention | Fits "only goose" | Needs one documented exception |

**Choice: River's native migrator**, which is simpler to maintain.

- **Where it runs.** `database.Migrate` (`internal/platform/database`) runs goose, then
  `rivermigrate` up, in the existing startup step of `cmd/seed` and `cmd/api`, so
  `databasetest` gets it too.
- **Worker check.** `cmd/worker` doesn't migrate. It calls the migrator's `Validate` and
  refuses to start on an outdated schema.
- **The exception.** CLAUDE.md's migration rule gains: "River's own tables are migrated by
  River's versioned migrator".
- **Upgrading River:**
  1. bump `river` and `riverdatabasesql` together;
  2. read River's changelog for migration notes;
  3. run `make test-integration`, which uses throwaway databases that run the migrations;
  4. deploy: the seed and API apply River's new migrations before the worker restarts and
     validates.

#### 6. API, unchanged from the proposal except the message status

- **`POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch`,** with an optional body
  `{"scheduled_at": "2026-11-20T09:00:00+03:00"}`.
  - `scheduled_at` must be in the future and at most a year ahead.
  - Names are re-checked through the existing `referenceChecker`.
  - Success: `202`, the campaign, and `Location: /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery`.
  - Errors: 409 `already_dispatched`, 404, 422 `invalid_campaign`, and 400/413 as today.
- **`GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery`:** the snapshot of section 3.
- **`GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery/events`:** SSE.
  - It recomputes every second, sends on change, and pings every 15 s.
  - It extends its write deadline before each write.
  - It stops on disconnect or shutdown (cmd/api passes its signal context) and after `done`.
  - It sets `X-Accel-Buffering: no` for nginx.
- **Routing.** `cmd/api` mounts these delivery routes beside the campaign handler. Chi
  routing for this is checked first; the fallback is the campaign router mounting a handler
  it is given.

#### 7. Schema (goose `migrations/00005_delivery.sql`)

- **Campaigns:**
  - the status CHECK gains `sent` and `failed`;
  - `scheduled_at`, `started_at` and `finished_at`, with CHECKs: `scheduled` ⇒
    `scheduled_at`; `sending`/`sent` ⇒ `started_at`; `sent`/`failed` ⇒ `finished_at`.
- **`deliveries`:**
  - `id`;
  - `campaign_id` and `customer_id`, with foreign keys;
  - `channel`, checked;
  - `status`, checked;
  - `provider_message_id`;
  - `error`, a reason code, never personal data;
  - `created_at` and `updated_at`.

  It has `UNIQUE (campaign_id, customer_id, channel)` and an index on
  `(campaign_id, status, channel)` for completion checks and snapshots.
- **`mock_provider_messages`:** `idempotency_key` as the primary key, `channel`,
  `provider_message_id`, `outcome`, `attempts` and the times. It is documented as the mock's
  storage, to drop with real providers.

#### 8. Layout

- **`internal/campaign`:**
  - lifecycle: `Dispatch`, `Start`, `Finish` and `Fail`, guarded by `CanBecome`;
  - `Fill` for placeholders;
  - `Store`:
    - `Dispatch`: lock, transition, conditional save and the job insert, through a
      consumer-declared `dispatcher`;
    - `Lock` and `Transition`, which delivery uses on its own transaction through
      `campaign.NewStore(tx)`;
  - the dispatch handler.
- **`internal/delivery` (new):**
  - `delivery.go`: package doc, `Queue` (the River wrapper), job arguments, channel settings;
  - `jobs.go`: the dispatch, send and receipt workers, and message completion;
  - `store.go`: deliveries, snapshot queries;
  - `mock.go`: the mock provider and its table;
  - `http.go`: snapshot and SSE.
- **`internal/platform/database`:** `Migrate` also runs `rivermigrate`.
- **`cmd/worker` (new):**
  - configuration, database and schema validation;
  - the advisory lock;
  - the River client with the three queues, then the mock providers and limiters;
  - graceful stop.
- **`compose.yaml`:** a `worker` service, started after `seed`, with
  `stop_grace_period: 15s`.
- **Makefile:** `make worker`.
- **New dependencies:** `river` and `riverdatabasesql` v0.51.2, and `golang.org/x/time`.

#### 9. Tests (point 9)

Unit tests use scripted fakes; integration tests use PostgreSQL and `rivertest`.

- **Concurrent completion:** 200 pending deliveries completed by 200 goroutines at once,
  repeated 20 times. The campaign reaches `sent` every time, with exactly one finish and none
  stuck.
- **Rate limit per channel:** 250 SMS and 250 email sends through the workers, with a fake
  provider recording timestamps. Some first attempts fail, so retries are counted. No
  one-second window exceeds 100 calls per channel.
- **SMS/email isolation:** the fake SMS provider blocks, and email deliveries still
  complete.
- **Idempotency after restart:** a second mock instance on the same database, given the same
  key, returns the same provider ID. There is one row and one receipt job, and the attempts
  were counted.
- **Atomic deliveries and jobs:**
  - A recipients fake that fails on the second batch leaves no deliveries and no send jobs.
  - On success, each delivery has exactly one send job in its channel's queue.
  - Running it again adds nothing.
- **Scheduled job recovery:**
  - A dispatch scheduled while no worker runs is worked once a worker starts after its time.
  - A dispatch interrupted mid-transaction leaves nothing behind and succeeds on retry.
- **Deterministic failures and receipts:** keys from each bucket end in their expected
  statuses (rejected, retried then delivered, timeout then the same acceptance, undelivered),
  identically across two runs.
- **Also:**
  - 20 concurrent dispatch requests give one 202 and nineteen 409s, with one dispatch job;
  - the dispatch handler's 202, 404, 409 and 422;
  - lifecycle transitions and `Fill`;
  - SSE ends exactly when `final`, and on cancellation;
  - the schema constraints;
  - the advisory lock: a second worker waits.

#### 10. Docs

- **DECISIONS.md:**
  - **24.** River: the native migrator and upgrade procedure, one active worker with
    standbys, queues and rate limits.
  - **25.** Delivery: transaction boundaries and locking, idempotency layers and the limits
    of exactly-once, the deterministic mock.
  - **26.** The dispatch and progress API, and the lifecycle with SSE termination.
  - **Open questions:** answer queue, real time and mock provider.
- **CLAUDE.md:** the River exception to the migration rule; `make worker`.
- **README.md:**
  - the API, the worker service and `make worker`;
  - further improvements: approving the reviewed version (If-Match, 412), unschedule and
    cancel, a receipt timeout, cross-campaign statistics, `/metrics`, real providers with
    webhooks, multi-part SMS, batched dispatch for very large audiences, authentication.
- **PHASE_REVIEWS.md:** the Phase 5 entry.

#### Verification

1. `make check` and `make test-integration`.
2. `docker compose up --build`, then through nginx:
   - dispatch the Phase 2A example on both channels;
   - `curl -N` the events stream until `done`;
   - check the snapshot against `CountAudience`;
   - check that `mock_provider_messages` holds one row per accepted message;
   - send 10 parallel dispatches and get one 202;
   - schedule a dispatch 2 minutes ahead and see it start on time;
   - restart the worker mid-send and see no message sent twice.
3. Delete the test campaigns afterwards; the customers still number 5,000.

```
1. Simplify worker deployment.

    * It does not mean every message has been delivered.
    * Delivery receipts may continue updating message statuses afterward.
```

**Plan presented for approval:**

### Phase 5 (revised): Delivery — dispatch, River queues, mock providers, live progress

#### Context

Approved campaigns must be sent asynchronously through mock providers, with delivery tracked
in real time. Phase 4 left:

- a lifecycle (`draft`, `scheduled`, `sending`);
- keyset `segmentation.Recipients`;
- the rule that permissions are re-checked just before sending.

**Decided so far:**

- one `POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch` (no `scheduled_at` means now);
- no approval precondition yet, noted in the README;
- SSE plus a snapshot, with statistics per campaign;
- River with native scheduled jobs (no cron);
- conditional transitions, 409 when already dispatched, and idempotency through constraints;
- a separate worker, in-process mock providers and delayed receipts;
- no authentication.

**Facts checked in River v0.51.2:**

- `riverdatabasesql.New(*sql.DB)` works jobs in poll-only mode on GORM's pool.
- `InsertTx` accepts GORM's `*sql.Tx`.
- `QueueConfig.MaxWorkers` is set per queue.
- `rivermigrate` provides `Migrate` and `Validate`.
- Unique jobs only hold while the job row exists; completed jobs are cleaned after 24 h.
- `JobCancel` and `JobSnooze` exist.

#### 1. Processes, queues, concurrency and rate limits (points 1 and 5)

**Exactly one worker process.** Docker Compose runs exactly one `worker`, and the rate
limiters live in it, independent for SMS and email. Multiple active worker replicas are
unsupported: each would bring its own limiters and break the global limits. DECISIONS.md,
the README and a comment in `compose.yaml` say so.

A second replica would add no throughput anyway, because the provider rate, not the worker,
is the bottleneck. There is no leader election or standby.

**Queues.**

| Queue | Jobs | Concurrency (`MaxWorkers`) | Rate limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| `sms` | SMS sends | 20 | 100 provider requests/s |
| `email` | email sends | 20 | 100 provider requests/s |
| `default` | dispatch, receipts | 10 | none (no provider call) |

- **Concurrency and rate are separate settings.** Concurrency caps the provider calls in
  flight. The rate caps how many calls start per second.
- **The rate limit covers every provider call, retries included.** The limit is a
  `golang.org/x/time/rate` limiter per channel, at 100/s with a burst of 1: calls are spaced
  at least 10 ms apart, so no one-second window holds more than 100. The send worker waits on
  its channel's limiter immediately before the call.
- **Channels are independent.** Each channel has its own queue, workers and limiter, so a
  slow or saturated SMS provider can't hold email.
- **Settings.** Concurrency and rate live in one per-channel table of code constants.

**The API** holds an insert-only River client on the same pool.

#### 2. Transactions and locking (points 2 and 4)

Every transaction is short, and none is open during a provider call. A transaction that
touches a campaign and its deliveries always locks the campaign row first (`SELECT … FOR
UPDATE`), so they can't deadlock.

**Dispatch request (API), one transaction:**

1. lock the campaign;
2. apply the domain transition `Dispatch(scheduledAt, now)`: a draft becomes `sending` or
   `scheduled`, anything else is `ErrNotDraft`, answered as 409 `already_dispatched`;
3. save it with a conditional update;
4. `InsertTx` the `dispatch` job: unique by campaign, with `ScheduledAt` when scheduled.

Concurrent requests queue on the row lock; the first wins and the rest get 409.

**`dispatch` job, one preparation transaction (point 4: no checkpoint, no extra table):**

Everything runs on one GORM transaction `tx`:

- the campaign through `campaign.NewStore(tx)`;
- recipient enumeration through `segmentation.NewService(tx)`;
- delivery inserts through the deliveries store on `tx`;
- River jobs through `InsertManyTx` with that transaction's `*sql.Tx`.

So every read and write of the preparation uses one connection, and commits or rolls back
together. The isolation level is READ COMMITTED: the audience isn't a snapshot (decision 22).

1. Lock the campaign. `scheduled` becomes `Start` (`sending`, `started_at`). `sending`
   continues, since this is the immediate case or a retry. Any other status means nothing to
   do.
2. For each batch of 1,000 from `Recipients` (with `AsOf = started_at`):
   - insert one `pending` delivery per recipient and channel (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
     RETURNING id`);
   - `InsertManyTx` their send jobs into their channel's queue.
3. With no deliveries at all, `Finish`.

Commit makes every delivery and its send job visible together, or nothing. No send can run
before the audience is complete, so "nothing pending" can't come early. A failed transaction
rolls back whole and River retries it.

An invalid segment instead runs a short transaction that marks the campaign `failed`, and
the job ends with `JobCancel`.

**The cost of one transaction is measured, not assumed.** An integration benchmark times
preparation on the seeded dataset (every customer: about 3,500 recipients and 4,500
deliveries and jobs). The result goes in DECISIONS.md with the scaling limit: the
transaction grows with the audience and holds the campaign row lock throughout. Audiences in
the hundreds of thousands would need batched preparation with a checkpoint; that is noted,
not built.

**Completing a message, one short transaction, after the provider call:**

1. lock the campaign row;
2. `UPDATE deliveries SET status = … WHERE id = ? AND status = 'pending'`; no row means it
   was already completed, so stop;
3. if no delivery of the campaign is `pending`, apply `Finish` (sending → sent, conditional);
4. commit.

The campaign lock serialises completions of one campaign. Each one starts only after the
previous one committed, and reads with a fresh snapshot. So the last completion always sees
zero pending, and no campaign can be left in `sending`. At 100 messages/s per channel, the
serialised transactions of about 1 ms each are far from a bottleneck.

**Receipt:** `UPDATE … WHERE id = ? AND status = 'accepted'`, with no campaign lock: receipts
never change a campaign's status. If the message is still `pending`, the job snoozes
(`JobSnooze`).

**Retry exhaustion (point 2).** River v0.51.2's own behaviour, read from its source:

- When `Work` returns an error or panics on an attempt where `Attempt >= MaxAttempts`, the
  job becomes `discarded`. `ErrorHandler` runs first, outside any transaction.
- A run lost to a crash is rescued after `RescueStuckJobsAfter`. It becomes `retryable` if
  attempts remain, and is otherwise discarded without any hook.

So no hook can record a failure atomically with River's discard. **The worker owns the retry
budget instead:**

- **The budget.** It is 5 provider attempts, the constant `maxSendAttempts`. River's
  `MaxAttempts` stays at its default of 25, which leaves headroom.
- **A transient failure on attempt 5** records `failed` with reason `provider_unavailable`,
  through the same completion transaction, and returns nil.
- **Any run after attempt 5,** which only follows a crash or panic during attempt 5, first
  records that failure, without calling the provider.

A send job therefore completes only after its delivery reached a terminal state, and campaign
completion runs as usual. River could discard a send job only after 20 further failed
attempts to record its outcome, which spread over weeks of retries; that is documented as the
bound.

`RescueStuckJobsAfter` is 10 minutes, above the longest job timeout (dispatch, 5 minutes), so
a crashed worker's jobs come back within minutes. A graceful stop already returns interrupted
jobs at once.

#### 3. Lifecycle and statuses (point 7)

- **Campaigns:**
  - `draft → scheduled → sending → sent`, and `scheduled` or `sending` → `failed`;
  - `scheduled → draft` stays in `CanBecome` for a later unschedule endpoint;
  - new times `scheduled_at`, `started_at` and `finished_at`.
- **Messages:**
  - `pending` → `accepted` (the provider took the request);
  - `accepted` → `delivered`, or `failed` with reason `undelivered`, from the receipt;
  - `pending` → `failed` (`rejected`, `too_long`, `provider_unavailable`) or `skipped`
    (permission revoked or contact detail gone).

  This renames the proposal's `sent` to `accepted`, so that provider acceptance is never
  read as delivery.
- **When a campaign is `sent`:** every provider send attempt has reached a terminal business
  outcome. No message is `pending`; each was accepted by its provider, failed (rejected, too
  long, or retries exhausted) or skipped.
- **`sent` doesn't mean delivered.** Receipts keep moving messages from `accepted` to
  `delivered` or `failed` afterwards. The snapshot keeps changing, and the campaign stays
  `sent`.
- **Snapshot:**
  - `status` and the three times;
  - `recipients`;
  - `messages` and `by_channel`, counting `pending`, `accepted`, `delivered`, `failed` and
    `skipped`;
  - `final`: true when the campaign is `sent` or `failed` and nothing is `pending` or
    `accepted`.
- **SSE ends exactly when `final` becomes true.** It sends `event: done` with the final
  snapshot and closes; the client closes its EventSource on `done`, since it would otherwise
  reconnect. A draft gets one snapshot and `done`. Receipts that never arrive from a real
  provider would need a receipt timeout, which is a further improvement.

#### 4. Provider idempotency and the deterministic mock (points 3 and 6)

**Interface.** The `provider` interface is declared by `internal/delivery`:
`Send(ctx, message) (providerID string, err error)`. The message carries an idempotency key,
`delivery-<id>`. Errors are permanent (a rejection) or transient (anything else, timeouts
included).

**Durable idempotency.** The mock keeps its own state in a `mock_provider_messages` table
keyed by idempotency key: provider ID, outcome, attempts, accepted time. That table stands in
for a real provider's database.

- A repeated call with the same key returns the stored result, and never schedules a second
  receipt, across restarts too.
- On first acceptance, the mock's own transaction stores the result and inserts the `receipt`
  job, unique by delivery. That job stands in for the provider's webhook. Acceptance and
  receipt are saved together, so a crash can't lose the receipt.
- River stays inside the mock: it holds its own insert-only River client. The `provider`
  interface knows nothing of River or receipts.

**Determinism.** The outcome is a pure function of the key: FNV-1a of the key, modulo 100.

| Bucket | Behaviour |
|---|---|
| 0 | Permanent rejection, so `failed` |
| 1 | Accepts, then answers after the 2 s call deadline on the first attempt. The retry returns the stored acceptance (the idempotency demonstration) |
| 2–3 | Transient error on the first attempt, accepted on the retry |
| 4–5 | Accepted; the receipt says undelivered |
| others | Accepted; the receipt says delivered |

Latency and receipt delay are fixed per channel: 50 ms and 2 s for SMS, 80 ms and 3 s for
email. The bucket function can be swapped, and worker tests use scripted fakes instead of
the mock.

**Limits of exactly-once (to record in DECISIONS):**

- Messages are sent at least once. Retries, crashes between the provider's acceptance and
  our commit, and timeouts can all repeat a call, and the stable idempotency key makes those
  repeats harmless only if the provider honours it.
- Real providers keep keys for a limited window, typically about 24 hours.
- Without key support, a duplicate is possible exactly in that crash gap.
- Carriers can still duplicate SMS, and receipts can be late, missing or wrong. So
  `delivered` is best effort.

#### 5. River migrations (point 8)

| | goose (River's SQL copied into migrations) | River's native migrator (`rivermigrate`) |
|---|---|---|
| History | One goose history | Our tables in goose; River's tracked in `river_migration` |
| Upgrade | Generate River's new SQL into a new goose file by hand; easy to forget | Bump the dependency; the next start applies River's migrations |
| Correctness | Our copy can drift from River's tested SQL | River's own tested, versioned SQL |
| Convention | Fits "only goose" | Needs one documented exception |

**Choice: River's native migrator**, which is simpler to maintain.

- **Where it runs.** `database.Migrate` (`internal/platform/database`) runs goose, then
  `rivermigrate` up, in the existing startup step of `cmd/seed` and `cmd/api`, so
  `databasetest` gets it too.
- **Worker check.** `cmd/worker` doesn't migrate. It calls the migrator's `Validate` and
  refuses to start on an outdated schema.
- **The exception.** CLAUDE.md's migration rule gains: "River's own tables are migrated by
  River's versioned migrator".
- **Upgrading River:**
  1. bump `river` and `riverdatabasesql` together;
  2. read River's changelog for migration notes;
  3. run `make test-integration`, which uses throwaway databases that run the migrations;
  4. deploy: the seed and API apply River's new migrations before the worker restarts and
     validates.

#### 6. API, unchanged from the proposal except the message status

- **`POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch`,** with an optional body
  `{"scheduled_at": "2026-11-20T09:00:00+03:00"}`.
  - `scheduled_at` must be in the future and at most a year ahead.
  - Names are re-checked through the existing `referenceChecker`.
  - Success: `202`, the campaign, and `Location: /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery`.
  - Errors: 409 `already_dispatched`, 404, 422 `invalid_campaign`, and 400/413 as today.
- **`GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery`:** the snapshot of section 3.
- **`GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery/events`:** SSE.
  - It recomputes every second, sends on change, and pings every 15 s.
  - It extends its write deadline before each write.
  - It stops on disconnect or shutdown (cmd/api passes its signal context) and after `done`.
  - It sets `X-Accel-Buffering: no` for nginx.
- **Routing.** `cmd/api` mounts these delivery routes beside the campaign handler. Chi
  routing for this is checked first; the fallback is the campaign router mounting a handler
  it is given.

#### 7. Schema (goose `migrations/00005_delivery.sql`)

- **Campaigns:**
  - the status CHECK gains `sent` and `failed`;
  - `scheduled_at`, `started_at` and `finished_at`, with CHECKs: `scheduled` ⇒
    `scheduled_at`; `sending`/`sent` ⇒ `started_at`; `sent`/`failed` ⇒ `finished_at`.
- **`deliveries`:**
  - `id`;
  - `campaign_id` and `customer_id`, with foreign keys;
  - `channel`, checked;
  - `status`, checked;
  - `provider_message_id`;
  - `error`, a reason code, never personal data;
  - `created_at` and `updated_at`.

  It has `UNIQUE (campaign_id, customer_id, channel)` and an index on
  `(campaign_id, status, channel)` for completion checks and snapshots.
- **`mock_provider_messages`:** `idempotency_key` as the primary key, `channel`,
  `provider_message_id`, `outcome`, `attempts` and the times. It is documented as the mock's
  storage, to drop with real providers.

#### 8. Layout

- **`internal/campaign`:**
  - lifecycle: `Dispatch`, `Start`, `Finish` and `Fail`, guarded by `CanBecome`;
  - `Fill` for placeholders;
  - `Store`:
    - `Dispatch`: lock, transition, conditional save and the job insert, through a
      consumer-declared `dispatcher`;
    - `Lock` and `Transition`, which delivery uses on its own transaction through
      `campaign.NewStore(tx)`;
  - the dispatch handler.
- **`internal/delivery` (new):**
  - `delivery.go`: package doc, `Queue` (the River wrapper), job arguments, channel settings;
  - `jobs.go`: the dispatch, send and receipt workers, and message completion;
  - `store.go`: deliveries, snapshot queries;
  - `mock.go`: the mock provider and its table;
  - `http.go`: snapshot and SSE.
- **`internal/platform/database`:** `Migrate` also runs `rivermigrate`.
- **`cmd/worker` (new):**
  - configuration, database and schema validation;
  - the River client with the three queues, then the mock providers and limiters;
  - graceful stop.
- **`compose.yaml`:** exactly one `worker` service, started after `seed`, with
  `stop_grace_period: 15s`, and a comment that it must not be scaled.
- **Makefile:** `make worker`.
- **New dependencies:** `river` and `riverdatabasesql` v0.51.2, and `golang.org/x/time`.

#### 9. Tests (point 9)

Unit tests use scripted fakes; integration tests use PostgreSQL and `rivertest`.

- **Concurrent completion:** 200 pending deliveries completed by 200 goroutines at once,
  repeated 20 times. The campaign reaches `sent` every time, with exactly one finish and none
  stuck.
- **Rate limit per channel:** 250 SMS and 250 email sends through the workers, with a fake
  provider recording timestamps. Some first attempts fail, so retries are counted. No
  one-second window exceeds 100 calls per channel.
- **SMS/email isolation:** the fake SMS provider blocks, and email deliveries still
  complete.
- **Idempotency after restart:** a second mock instance on the same database, given the same
  key, returns the same provider ID. There is one row and one receipt job, and the attempts
  were counted.
- **Atomic deliveries and jobs:**
  - A recipients fake that fails on the second batch leaves no deliveries and no send jobs.
  - On success, each delivery has exactly one send job in its channel's queue.
  - Running it again adds nothing.
- **Scheduled job recovery:**
  - A dispatch scheduled while no worker runs is worked once a worker starts after its time.
  - A dispatch interrupted mid-transaction leaves nothing behind and succeeds on retry.
- **Deterministic failures and receipts:** keys from each bucket end in their expected
  statuses (rejected, retried then delivered, timeout then the same acceptance, undelivered),
  identically across two runs.
- **Retry exhaustion:**
  - A provider that always fails transiently leaves the delivery `failed`
    (`provider_unavailable`) after 5 attempts.
  - A run after attempt 5, as after a crash, records the failure without calling the
    provider.
  - The campaign still reaches `sent`.
- **Preparation timing:** a benchmark of the dispatch transaction on the seeded dataset.
- **Also:**
  - 20 concurrent dispatch requests give one 202 and nineteen 409s, with one dispatch job;
  - the dispatch handler's 202, 404, 409 and 422;
  - lifecycle transitions and `Fill`;
  - SSE ends exactly when `final`, and on cancellation;
  - the schema constraints.

#### 10. Docs

- **DECISIONS.md:**
  - **24.** River: the native migrator and upgrade procedure; exactly one worker, since
    several replicas would break the rate limits; queues and rate limits; retry exhaustion.
  - **25.** Delivery: transaction boundaries and locking, the measured preparation cost and
    its scaling limit, idempotency layers and the limits of exactly-once, the deterministic
    mock.
  - **26.** The dispatch and progress API, and the lifecycle with SSE termination.
  - **Open questions:** answer queue, real time and mock provider.
- **CLAUDE.md:** the River exception to the migration rule; `make worker`.
- **README.md:**
  - the API, the worker service and `make worker`;
  - further improvements: approving the reviewed version (If-Match, 412), unschedule and
    cancel, a receipt timeout, cross-campaign statistics, `/metrics`, real providers with
    webhooks, multi-part SMS, batched dispatch for very large audiences, authentication.
- **PHASE_REVIEWS.md:** the Phase 5 entry.

#### Verification

1. `make check` and `make test-integration`.
2. `docker compose up --build`, then through nginx:
   - dispatch the Phase 2A example on both channels;
   - `curl -N` the events stream until `done`;
   - check the snapshot against `CountAudience`;
   - check that `mock_provider_messages` holds one row per accepted message;
   - send 10 parallel dispatches and get one 202;
   - schedule a dispatch 2 minutes ahead and see it start on time;
   - restart the worker mid-send and see no message sent twice.
3. Measure the preparation benchmark before writing its numbers into DECISIONS.md.
4. Delete the test campaigns afterwards; the customers still number 5,000.

```
## Approved Plan:
# Phase 5 (revised): Delivery — dispatch, River queues, mock providers, live progress

## Context

Approved campaigns must be sent asynchronously through mock providers, with delivery tracked
in real time. Phase 4 left:

- a lifecycle (`draft`, `scheduled`, `sending`);
- keyset `segmentation.Recipients`;
- the rule that permissions are re-checked just before sending.

**Decided so far:**

- one `POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch` (no `scheduled_at` means now);
- no approval precondition yet, noted in the README;
- SSE plus a snapshot, with statistics per campaign;
- River with native scheduled jobs (no cron);
- conditional transitions, 409 when already dispatched, and idempotency through constraints;
- a separate worker, in-process mock providers and delayed receipts;
- no authentication.

**Facts checked in River v0.51.2:**

- `riverdatabasesql.New(*sql.DB)` works jobs in poll-only mode on GORM's pool.
- `InsertTx` accepts GORM's `*sql.Tx`.
- `QueueConfig.MaxWorkers` is set per queue.
- `rivermigrate` provides `Migrate` and `Validate`.
- Unique jobs only hold while the job row exists; completed jobs are cleaned after 24 h.
- `JobCancel` and `JobSnooze` exist.

## 1. Processes, queues, concurrency and rate limits (points 1 and 5)

**Exactly one worker process.** Docker Compose runs exactly one `worker`, and the rate
limiters live in it, independent for SMS and email. Multiple active worker replicas are
unsupported: each would bring its own limiters and break the global limits. DECISIONS.md,
the README and a comment in `compose.yaml` say so.

A second replica would add no throughput anyway, because the provider rate, not the worker,
is the bottleneck. There is no leader election or standby.

**Queues.**

| Queue | Jobs | Concurrency (`MaxWorkers`) | Rate limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| `sms` | SMS sends | 20 | 100 provider requests/s |
| `email` | email sends | 20 | 100 provider requests/s |
| `default` | dispatch, receipts | 10 | none (no provider call) |

- **Concurrency and rate are separate settings.** Concurrency caps the provider calls in
  flight. The rate caps how many calls start per second.
- **The rate limit covers every provider call, retries included.** The limit is a
  `golang.org/x/time/rate` limiter per channel, at 100/s with a burst of 1: calls are spaced
  at least 10 ms apart, so no one-second window holds more than 100. The send worker waits on
  its channel's limiter immediately before the call.
- **Channels are independent.** Each channel has its own queue, workers and limiter, so a
  slow or saturated SMS provider can't hold email.
- **Settings.** Concurrency and rate live in one per-channel table of code constants.

**The API** holds an insert-only River client on the same pool.

## 2. Transactions and locking (points 2 and 4)

Every transaction is short, and none is open during a provider call. A transaction that
touches a campaign and its deliveries always locks the campaign row first (`SELECT … FOR
UPDATE`), so they can't deadlock.

**Dispatch request (API), one transaction:**

1. lock the campaign;
2. apply the domain transition `Dispatch(scheduledAt, now)`: a draft becomes `sending` or
   `scheduled`, anything else is `ErrNotDraft`, answered as 409 `already_dispatched`;
3. save it with a conditional update;
4. `InsertTx` the `dispatch` job: unique by campaign, with `ScheduledAt` when scheduled.

Concurrent requests queue on the row lock; the first wins and the rest get 409.

**`dispatch` job, one preparation transaction (point 4: no checkpoint, no extra table):**

Everything runs on one GORM transaction `tx`:

- the campaign through `campaign.NewStore(tx)`;
- recipient enumeration through `segmentation.NewService(tx)`;
- delivery inserts through the deliveries store on `tx`;
- River jobs through `InsertManyTx` with that transaction's `*sql.Tx`.

So every read and write of the preparation uses one connection, and commits or rolls back
together. The isolation level is READ COMMITTED: the audience isn't a snapshot (decision 22).

1. Lock the campaign. `scheduled` becomes `Start` (`sending`, `started_at`). `sending`
   continues, since this is the immediate case or a retry. Any other status means nothing to
   do.
2. For each batch of 1,000 from `Recipients` (with `AsOf = started_at`):
   - insert one `pending` delivery per recipient and channel (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
     RETURNING id`);
   - `InsertManyTx` their send jobs into their channel's queue.
3. With no deliveries at all, `Finish`.

Commit makes every delivery and its send job visible together, or nothing. No send can run
before the audience is complete, so "nothing pending" can't come early. A failed transaction
rolls back whole and River retries it.

An invalid segment instead runs a short transaction that marks the campaign `failed`, and
the job ends with `JobCancel`.

**The cost of one transaction is measured, not assumed.** An integration benchmark times
preparation on the seeded dataset (every customer: about 3,500 recipients and 4,500
deliveries and jobs). The result goes in DECISIONS.md with the scaling limit: the
transaction grows with the audience and holds the campaign row lock throughout. Audiences in
the hundreds of thousands would need batched preparation with a checkpoint; that is noted,
not built.

**Completing a message, one short transaction, after the provider call:**

1. lock the campaign row;
2. `UPDATE deliveries SET status = … WHERE id = ? AND status = 'pending'`; no row means it
   was already completed, so stop;
3. if no delivery of the campaign is `pending`, apply `Finish` (sending → sent, conditional);
4. commit.

The campaign lock serialises completions of one campaign. Each one starts only after the
previous one committed, and reads with a fresh snapshot. So the last completion always sees
zero pending, and no campaign can be left in `sending`. At 100 messages/s per channel, the
serialised transactions of about 1 ms each are far from a bottleneck.

**Receipt:** `UPDATE … WHERE id = ? AND status = 'accepted'`, with no campaign lock: receipts
never change a campaign's status. If the message is still `pending`, the job snoozes
(`JobSnooze`).

**Retry exhaustion (point 2).** River v0.51.2's own behaviour, read from its source:

- When `Work` returns an error or panics on an attempt where `Attempt >= MaxAttempts`, the
  job becomes `discarded`. `ErrorHandler` runs first, outside any transaction.
- A run lost to a crash is rescued after `RescueStuckJobsAfter`. It becomes `retryable` if
  attempts remain, and is otherwise discarded without any hook.

So no hook can record a failure atomically with River's discard. **The worker owns the retry
budget instead:**

- **The budget.** It is 5 provider attempts, the constant `maxSendAttempts`. River's
  `MaxAttempts` stays at its default of 25, which leaves headroom.
- **A transient failure on attempt 5** records `failed` with reason `provider_unavailable`,
  through the same completion transaction, and returns nil.
- **Any run after attempt 5,** which only follows a crash or panic during attempt 5, first
  records that failure, without calling the provider.

A send job therefore completes only after its delivery reached a terminal state, and campaign
completion runs as usual. River could discard a send job only after 20 further failed
attempts to record its outcome, which spread over weeks of retries; that is documented as the
bound.

`RescueStuckJobsAfter` is 10 minutes, above the longest job timeout (dispatch, 5 minutes), so
a crashed worker's jobs come back within minutes. A graceful stop already returns interrupted
jobs at once.

## 3. Lifecycle and statuses (point 7)

- **Campaigns:**
  - `draft → scheduled → sending → sent`, and `scheduled` or `sending` → `failed`;
  - `scheduled → draft` stays in `CanBecome` for a later unschedule endpoint;
  - new times `scheduled_at`, `started_at` and `finished_at`.
- **Messages:**
  - `pending` → `accepted` (the provider took the request);
  - `accepted` → `delivered`, or `failed` with reason `undelivered`, from the receipt;
  - `pending` → `failed` (`rejected`, `too_long`, `provider_unavailable`) or `skipped`
    (permission revoked or contact detail gone).

  This renames the proposal's `sent` to `accepted`, so that provider acceptance is never
  read as delivery.
- **When a campaign is `sent`:** every provider send attempt has reached a terminal business
  outcome. No message is `pending`; each was accepted by its provider, failed (rejected, too
  long, or retries exhausted) or skipped.
- **`sent` doesn't mean delivered.** Receipts keep moving messages from `accepted` to
  `delivered` or `failed` afterwards. The snapshot keeps changing, and the campaign stays
  `sent`.
- **Snapshot:**
  - `status` and the three times;
  - `recipients`;
  - `messages` and `by_channel`, counting `pending`, `accepted`, `delivered`, `failed` and
    `skipped`;
  - `final`: true when the campaign is `sent` or `failed` and nothing is `pending` or
    `accepted`.
- **SSE ends exactly when `final` becomes true.** It sends `event: done` with the final
  snapshot and closes; the client closes its EventSource on `done`, since it would otherwise
  reconnect. A draft gets one snapshot and `done`. Receipts that never arrive from a real
  provider would need a receipt timeout, which is a further improvement.

## 4. Provider idempotency and the deterministic mock (points 3 and 6)

**Interface.** The `provider` interface is declared by `internal/delivery`:
`Send(ctx, message) (providerID string, err error)`. The message carries an idempotency key,
`delivery-<id>`. Errors are permanent (a rejection) or transient (anything else, timeouts
included).

**Durable idempotency.** The mock keeps its own state in a `mock_provider_messages` table
keyed by idempotency key: provider ID, outcome, attempts, accepted time. That table stands in
for a real provider's database.

- A repeated call with the same key returns the stored result, and never schedules a second
  receipt, across restarts too.
- On first acceptance, the mock's own transaction stores the result and inserts the `receipt`
  job, unique by delivery. That job stands in for the provider's webhook. Acceptance and
  receipt are saved together, so a crash can't lose the receipt.
- River stays inside the mock: it holds its own insert-only River client. The `provider`
  interface knows nothing of River or receipts.

**Determinism.** The outcome is a pure function of the key: FNV-1a of the key, modulo 100.

| Bucket | Behaviour |
|---|---|
| 0 | Permanent rejection, so `failed` |
| 1 | Accepts, then answers after the 2 s call deadline on the first attempt. The retry returns the stored acceptance (the idempotency demonstration) |
| 2–3 | Transient error on the first attempt, accepted on the retry |
| 4–5 | Accepted; the receipt says undelivered |
| others | Accepted; the receipt says delivered |

Latency and receipt delay are fixed per channel: 50 ms and 2 s for SMS, 80 ms and 3 s for
email. The bucket function can be swapped, and worker tests use scripted fakes instead of
the mock.

**Limits of exactly-once (to record in DECISIONS):**

- Messages are sent at least once. Retries, crashes between the provider's acceptance and
  our commit, and timeouts can all repeat a call, and the stable idempotency key makes those
  repeats harmless only if the provider honours it.
- Real providers keep keys for a limited window, typically about 24 hours.
- Without key support, a duplicate is possible exactly in that crash gap.
- Carriers can still duplicate SMS, and receipts can be late, missing or wrong. So
  `delivered` is best effort.

## 5. River migrations (point 8)

| | goose (River's SQL copied into migrations) | River's native migrator (`rivermigrate`) |
|---|---|---|
| History | One goose history | Our tables in goose; River's tracked in `river_migration` |
| Upgrade | Generate River's new SQL into a new goose file by hand; easy to forget | Bump the dependency; the next start applies River's migrations |
| Correctness | Our copy can drift from River's tested SQL | River's own tested, versioned SQL |
| Convention | Fits "only goose" | Needs one documented exception |

**Choice: River's native migrator**, which is simpler to maintain.

- **Where it runs.** `database.Migrate` (`internal/platform/database`) runs goose, then
  `rivermigrate` up, in the existing startup step of `cmd/seed` and `cmd/api`, so
  `databasetest` gets it too.
- **Worker check.** `cmd/worker` doesn't migrate. It calls the migrator's `Validate` and
  refuses to start on an outdated schema.
- **The exception.** CLAUDE.md's migration rule gains: "River's own tables are migrated by
  River's versioned migrator".
- **Upgrading River:**
  1. bump `river` and `riverdatabasesql` together;
  2. read River's changelog for migration notes;
  3. run `make test-integration`, which uses throwaway databases that run the migrations;
  4. deploy: the seed and API apply River's new migrations before the worker restarts and
     validates.

## 6. API, unchanged from the proposal except the message status

- **`POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch`,** with an optional body
  `{"scheduled_at": "2026-11-20T09:00:00+03:00"}`.
  - `scheduled_at` must be in the future and at most a year ahead.
  - Names are re-checked through the existing `referenceChecker`.
  - Success: `202`, the campaign, and `Location: /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery`.
  - Errors: 409 `already_dispatched`, 404, 422 `invalid_campaign`, and 400/413 as today.
- **`GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery`:** the snapshot of section 3.
- **`GET /api/campaigns/{id}/delivery/events`:** SSE.
  - It recomputes every second, sends on change, and pings every 15 s.
  - It extends its write deadline before each write.
  - It stops on disconnect or shutdown (cmd/api passes its signal context) and after `done`.
  - It sets `X-Accel-Buffering: no` for nginx.
- **Routing.** `cmd/api` mounts these delivery routes beside the campaign handler. Chi
  routing for this is checked first; the fallback is the campaign router mounting a handler
  it is given.

## 7. Schema (goose `migrations/00005_delivery.sql`)

- **Campaigns:**
  - the status CHECK gains `sent` and `failed`;
  - `scheduled_at`, `started_at` and `finished_at`, with CHECKs: `scheduled` ⇒
    `scheduled_at`; `sending`/`sent` ⇒ `started_at`; `sent`/`failed` ⇒ `finished_at`.
- **`deliveries`:**
  - `id`;
  - `campaign_id` and `customer_id`, with foreign keys;
  - `channel`, checked;
  - `status`, checked;
  - `provider_message_id`;
  - `error`, a reason code, never personal data;
  - `created_at` and `updated_at`.

  It has `UNIQUE (campaign_id, customer_id, channel)` and an index on
  `(campaign_id, status, channel)` for completion checks and snapshots.
- **`mock_provider_messages`:** `idempotency_key` as the primary key, `channel`,
  `provider_message_id`, `outcome`, `attempts` and the times. It is documented as the mock's
  storage, to drop with real providers.

## 8. Layout

- **`internal/campaign`:**
  - lifecycle: `Dispatch`, `Start`, `Finish` and `Fail`, guarded by `CanBecome`;
  - `Fill` for placeholders;
  - `Store`:
    - `Dispatch`: lock, transition, conditional save and the job insert, through a
      consumer-declared `dispatcher`;
    - `Lock` and `Transition`, which delivery uses on its own transaction through
      `campaign.NewStore(tx)`;
  - the dispatch handler.
- **`internal/delivery` (new):**
  - `delivery.go`: package doc, `Queue` (the River wrapper), job arguments, channel settings;
  - `jobs.go`: the dispatch, send and receipt workers, and message completion;
  - `store.go`: deliveries, snapshot queries;
  - `mock.go`: the mock provider and its table;
  - `http.go`: snapshot and SSE.
- **`internal/platform/database`:** `Migrate` also runs `rivermigrate`.
- **`cmd/worker` (new):**
  - configuration, database and schema validation;
  - the River client with the three queues, then the mock providers and limiters;
  - graceful stop.
- **`compose.yaml`:** exactly one `worker` service, started after `seed`, with
  `stop_grace_period: 15s`, and a comment that it must not be scaled.
- **Makefile:** `make worker`.
- **New dependencies:** `river` and `riverdatabasesql` v0.51.2, and `golang.org/x/time`.

## 9. Tests (point 9)

Unit tests use scripted fakes; integration tests use PostgreSQL and `rivertest`.

- **Concurrent completion:** 200 pending deliveries completed by 200 goroutines at once,

  - Running it again adds nothing.
- **Scheduled job recovery:**
  - A dispatch scheduled while no worker runs is worked once a worker starts after its time.
  - A dispatch interrupted mid-transaction leaves nothing behind and succeeds on retry.
```

```
// Start starts the client's job fetching and working loops. Once this is called,
// the client will run in a background goroutine until stopped. All jobs are
// run with a context inheriting from the provided context, but with a timeout
// deadline applied based on the job's settings.
//
// A graceful shutdown stops fetching new jobs but allows any previously fetched
// jobs to complete. This can be initiated with the Stop method.
```

The migration first: dispatch times and the two new statuses on `campaigns`, the `deliveries` table, and the mock provider's own idempotency records. Each column's purpose is documented in the SQL.

**Write** `<repo>/migrations/00005_delivery.sql` (62 lines)

```
-- Delivery context: a dispatched campaign becomes one message per recipient and channel, sent
-- by the worker through the channel's provider. Campaigns gain the times and statuses of
-- dispatch. River's own job tables are migrated by River's migrator, not here (DECISIONS.md).

-- +goose Up
ALTER TABLE campaigns DROP CONSTRAINT campaigns_status_check;
ALTER TABLE campaigns
    ADD CONSTRAINT campaigns_status_check CHECK (status IN ('draft', 'scheduled', 'sending', 'sent', 'failed')),
    ADD COLUMN scheduled_at timestamptz, -- when a scheduled campaign is due
    ADD COLUMN started_at timestamptz,   -- when its dispatch began: the end of its segment's windows
    ADD COLUMN finished_at timestamptz,  -- when every message reached an outcome, or the dispatch failed
    ADD CONSTRAINT campaigns_scheduled_at_check CHECK (status <> 'scheduled' OR scheduled_at IS NOT NULL),
… [2,937 more characters]
```

```
// schedule moves a draft to scheduled behind the store's back, as a stale copy would not know.
func schedule(t *testing.T, db *gorm.DB, id int64) {
	t.Helper()
	if err := db.Exec("UPDATE campaigns SET status = ?, scheduled_at = ? WHERE id = ?", StatusScheduled, now.Add(time.Hour), id).Error; err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("schedule: %v", err)
	}
}
```

```
// Campaign is the aggregate root: a draft and its place in the lifecycle. It copies what it is
// given and what it returns, so it changes only through its methods.
type Campaign struct {
	id          int64
	draft       Draft
	status      Status
	createdAt   time.Time
	updatedAt   time.Time // when the draft last changed
	scheduledAt time.Time // zero unless it was scheduled
	startedAt   time.Time // zero until its dispatch starts
	finishedAt  time.Time // zero until it is sent or fails
}
```

**Edit** `<repo>/internal/campaign/campaign.go`

```
// Dispatch approves the draft for sending: now, or at scheduledAt when it is set, which must be
// in the future and at most a year ahead. It fails with ErrNotDraft unless the campaign is a
// draft, and with a *ValidationError for a time it can't be scheduled at.
func (c *Campaign) Dispatch(scheduledAt *time.Time, now time.Time) error {
	if err := c.ensureDraft(); err != nil {
		return err
	}
	if scheduledAt == nil {
		c.status, c.startedAt = StatusSending, now
		return nil
	}
	at := scheduledAt.UTC().Truncate(time.Microsecond) // PostgreSQL's precision
… [1,086 more characters]
```

**Edit** `<repo>/internal/campaign/campaign.go`

```
// UpdatedAt returns when the campaign's draft last changed.
func (c *Campaign) UpdatedAt() time.Time { return c.updatedAt }

// ScheduledAt returns when a scheduled campaign is due, or zero.
func (c *Campaign) ScheduledAt() time.Time { return c.scheduledAt }

// StartedAt returns when the campaign's dispatch started, or zero. Its segment's windows end
// then.
func (c *Campaign) StartedAt() time.Time { return c.startedAt }

// FinishedAt returns when the campaign was sent or failed, or zero.
func (c *Campaign) FinishedAt() time.Time { return c.finishedAt }
```

```
// SQLTx returns the database/sql transaction behind a GORM transaction, so that libraries
// such as River can take part in it. tx must come from (*gorm.DB).Transaction.
func SQLTx(tx *gorm.DB) (*sql.Tx, error) {
	sqlTx, ok := tx.Statement.ConnPool.(*sql.Tx)
	if !ok {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("not in a database/sql transaction: %T", tx.Statement.ConnPool)
	}
	return sqlTx, nil
}
```

```
// Dispatch saves a draft's move to scheduled or sending, which c already made, and lets
// enqueue queue its job in the same transaction: both happen or neither does. The write only
// applies while the stored campaign is a draft, so of several concurrent dispatches exactly
// one succeeds; the others get ErrNotDraft.
func (s *Store) Dispatch(ctx context.Context, c *Campaign, enqueue func(tx *sql.Tx) error) error {
	return s.db.WithContext(ctx).Transaction(func(tx *gorm.DB) error {
		result := tx.Model(&campaignRow{}).
			Where("id = ? AND status = ?", c.id, StatusDraft).
			Updates(statusColumns(c))
		if result.Error != nil {
			return fmt.Errorf("dispatch campaign %d: %w", c.id, result.Error)
		}
```

```
// NewHandler returns the campaign API, to be mounted at /api/campaigns: drafts, and their
// dispatch. Delivery progress is served by the delivery capability.
func NewHandler(svc *Service, logger *slog.Logger) http.Handler {
	h := &handler{svc: svc, logger: logger}
	r := chi.NewRouter()
	r.Post("/", h.create)
	r.Get("/", h.list)
	r.Get("/{id}", h.get)
	r.Put("/{id}", h.update)
	r.Delete("/{id}", h.delete)
	r.Post("/{id}/dispatch", h.dispatch)
```

```
// and Location points at its progress.

	id, ok := h.id(w, r)
	if !ok {
		return
	}
	var body dispatchJSON
	if err := httpserver.DecodeJSON(w, r, &body); err != nil && !errors.Is(err, io.EOF) { // no body: send now

		return
	}
```

```
	// switch a message to UCS-2, where one message holds only 70 characters (DECISIONS.md).
	maxSMSLength          = 160
	maxEmailSubjectLength = 80
)
```

The campaign package builds; the diagnostic predated the `sed` fix. Now the new `internal/delivery` package, starting with its package doc, job arguments, settings and the API-side queue:

**Write** `<repo>/internal/delivery/delivery.go` (108 lines)

```
// Package delivery sends dispatched campaigns. Preparing a campaign turns its audience into one
// pending message per recipient and channel, each with its own send job. A send job hands its
// message to the channel's provider at a bounded rate, and a receipt job records what the
// provider reports later. Progress is served as snapshots and server-sent events.
//
// The jobs run on River, a job queue in PostgreSQL. The API inserts them; exactly one worker
// process (cmd/worker) works them, because the per-channel rate limits live in that process.
// Like segmentation, delivery reads the customer tables directly.
```

Now the wiring. In `cmd/api`, the queue becomes the campaign service's dispatcher, and the delivery routes are mounted beside the campaign API. The shutdown context is handed to the SSE streams.

**Edit** `<repo>/cmd/api/main.go`

```
	queue, err := delivery.NewQueue(sqlDB, logger)
	if err != nil {
		return err
	}
	segments := segmentation.NewService(db)
	campaigns := campaign.NewService(campaign.NewStore(db), segments, queue, time.Now)
	// Event streams end with ctx: a server shutdown doesn't cancel the requests it waits for.
	router.Mount("/api/campaigns/{id}/delivery", delivery.NewHandler(campaigns, db, ctx, logger))
	router.Mount("/api/campaigns", campaign.NewHandler(campaigns, logger))
```

```
// Command worker sends dispatched campaigns: it works the delivery jobs the API queues.
//
// Exactly one worker may run. The per-channel provider rate limits live in its process, so a
// second worker would double them; multiple active replicas are not supported. It applies no
// migrations: the seed and api commands do, and it refuses to start on an outdated schema.
```

```
		t.Errorf("a second run answered %+v, want the same as the first", second)
	}
}
```

```
// Start begins sending a scheduled campaign whose time has come. A draft goes through Dispatch
// instead.
func (c *Campaign) Start(now time.Time) error {
	if c.status != StatusScheduled {
		return fmt.Errorf("%w: only a scheduled campaign starts, not a %s one", ErrStatusChange, c.status)
	}
	return c.moveTo(StatusSending, &c.startedAt, now)
}
```

**Bash** — Mutation-test completion locking, rate limiting and the retry budget

```sh
cd "<repo>" && SCR="/private/tmp/claude-502/<project>/61d394fd-a454-4187-b6af-7b54c26af34f/scratchpad/mutants5" && rm -rf "$SCR" && mkdir -p "$SCR" && cp internal/delivery/jobs.go "$SCR/jobs.go" && trap 'cp "$SCR/jobs.go" internal/delivery/jobs.go' EXIT
export TEST_DATABASE_URL='postgres://campaign:[REDACTED]@localhost:5432/campaign?sslmode=disable'
mutate() {
  name="$1" expr="$2" tests="$3"
  cp "$SCR/jobs.go" internal/delivery/jobs.go
  perl -0pi -e "$expr" internal/delivery/jobs.go
  if cmp -s "$SCR/jobs.go" internal/delivery/jobs.go; then echo "### $name: MUTATION DID NOT APPLY"; return; fi
  echo "### $name"
  go test -count=1 -run "$tests" ./internal/delivery 2>&1 | grep -E "^\s*--- FAIL|_test.go:[0-9]+|^(ok|FAIL)|build failed" | head -4
}
mutate "completion without the campaign lock" 's/(func completeMessage.*?)c, err := campaigns\.Lock\(ctx, m\.campaignID\)/$1c, err := campaigns.Get(ctx, m.campaignID)/s' 'TestCompletionIsRaceFree'
mutate "no rate limiter" 's/if err := w\.limiters\[m\.channel\]\.Wait\(ctx\); err != nil \{/if err := error(nil); err != nil {/' 'TestRateLimitPerChannel'
mutate "provider called past the budget" 's/if job\.Attempt <= maxSendAttempts \{/if true {/' 'TestRetryExhaustionFailsTheMessage'
cp "$SCR/jobs.go" internal/delivery/jobs.go && cmp "$SCR/jobs.go" internal/delivery/jobs.go && echo "jobs.go restored"
```

**Edit** `<repo>/DECISIONS.md`

```
## 24. Job queue: River on PostgreSQL, worked by one process

**Context.** Approved campaigns are sent asynchronously, scheduled ones at their time.
Decision 11 left the queue open between a PostgreSQL-backed queue and Redis Streams. The
providers allow 100 requests per second per channel.

**Decision.**

- **River** (riverqueue/river v0.51.2) runs on the existing PostgreSQL, through its
  `database/sql` driver on GORM's pool.
  - That driver can't LISTEN, so River runs in poll-only mode; it polls every 200 ms.
  - The API inserts a dispatch job in the same transaction as the campaign's status change
… [10,550 more characters]
```

**Edit** `<repo>/DECISIONS.md`

```
- **Real-time status:** answered by decision 26: server-sent events, each stream reading the
  database, so several API instances need no LISTEN/NOTIFY.
```

**Edit** `<repo>/DECISIONS.md`

```
- **Mock provider:** answered by decision 25: in-process adapters in the worker, with
  durable idempotency in their own table.
```

```
- [ ] **Optimistic locking for campaign drafts.** Concurrent edits are last-write-wins today.
- [ ] **Dispatch exactly what was reviewed.** Dispatch could take the version the reviewer
      approved (`If-Match` with the campaign's ETag) and answer 412 if the draft changed since.
      Today an edit made after review is sent as edited.
- [ ] **Unschedule and cancel.** The lifecycle allows scheduled → draft, but no endpoint does it
      yet, and a dispatch in progress can't be stopped.
- [ ] **Receipt timeout.** A real provider can lose a receipt, which would leave its message
      `accepted` and the progress never final.
- [ ] **Cross-campaign delivery statistics,** for a dashboard; statistics are per campaign today.
- [ ] **Real providers,** with receipts by webhook instead of the mocks' scheduled jobs.

      may run today, because each worker process holds its own limiters
```

Phase 5 is implemented and verified, and nothing is committed yet. Your five final adjustments are in:

1. **One worker.** Compose runs exactly one worker, with no leader election, and each channel has its own rate limiter in that process. The compose file, README, DECISIONS.md and CLAUDE.md all say a second active replica is unsupported, because it would double the limits.
2. **Retry exhaustion.** I read River's source: when a job's last attempt errors or panics, River discards it, and no hook can record a failure in the same transaction. A job lost to a crash with no attempts left is discarded silently. So the worker owns the budget: 5 provider attempts, while River keeps its default of 25. A failure on attempt 5, or any later run (which only happens after a crash), marks the message failed and lets the campaign complete. A send job can therefore only end after its message has an outcome.
3. **Crash-safe receipts.** The mock provider records its answer and schedules the receipt in one transaction of its own. The provider interface knows nothing of River.
4. **Transaction boundaries.** Reading the audience, inserting the deliveries and inserting the send jobs all run on one database transaction. I measured instead of assuming: preparing all 4,546 messages for every seeded customer takes 265–291 ms, about 60 µs per message. The scaling limit is documented.
5. **What `sent` means.** Every send attempt has a final outcome: accepted by the provider, failed, or skipped. It doesn't mean delivered, and receipts can still update messages afterwards. The live stream ends only once nothing can change any more.

For B, I read "add id to readme" as "add it to the README". Checking that the dispatched version is the reviewed one (an `If-Match` precondition) is now a further improvement there, next to authentication.

#### Verification
- **Test suites:** `make check` passes with lint at 0 issues, and so does `make test-integration` with the race detector. That includes every test you listed.
- **Mutation check:** I deliberately removed the campaign lock, the rate limiter and the retry budget, one at a time, and the matching test failed each time. Without the lock, a campaign stayed stuck in `sending` in the very first round.
- **Running stack, through nginx:**
  - a dispatch streamed live until it finished;
  - of 10 parallel dispatches, one was accepted and nine got `409`;
  - the worker was restarted halfway through 4,546 messages, and nothing was sent twice: 4,512 accepted messages matched exactly 4,512 records in the mock;
  - a scheduled dispatch started 27 ms after its time, with no cron.
- **Cleanup:** I deleted the test campaigns afterwards; the database still has its 5,000 customers.

#### Fixes made along the way
- A test showed that `Start` could start a draft, skipping dispatch's checks. It now only starts scheduled campaigns.
- A receipt that arrives before its message's acceptance is recorded is retried at most 5 times, instead of being postponed without limit.

#### Worth knowing
- River promotes scheduled jobs every 5 seconds, so a scheduled dispatch or a receipt can arrive up to 5 seconds late.
- The single-worker rule is documented and configured, but nothing stops someone from scaling the worker.

#### Proposed commits
I checked that each one builds and passes the integration tests on its own:

| # | Message | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `build: add the River job queue and run its migrations at startup` | `go.mod`, `go.sum`, `internal/platform/database/database.go` |
| 2 | `feat: dispatch campaigns and deliver them through River with mock providers` | migration `00005` and its tests, `internal/campaign`, `internal/delivery`, `cmd/api`, `cmd/worker`, `compose.yaml`, `Makefile` |
| 3 | `docs: record phase 5 delivery decisions and review` | `CLAUDE.md`, `DECISIONS.md`, `README.md`, `PHASE_REVIEWS.md` |

Commit 1 already lists `golang.org/x/time` in `go.mod`, though nothing uses it until commit 2. It's harmless, but `go mod tidy` would drop it at that point in the history.

Shall I commit?

_(pasted)_

func completeMessage(ctx context.Context, db *gorm.DB, m pendingMessage, out outcome, now time.Time) error {
	return db.WithContext(ctx).Transaction(func(tx *gorm.DB) error {
		campaigns := campaign.NewStore(tx)
		c, err := campaigns.Lock(ctx, m.campaignID)
		if err != nil {
			return err
		}
		deliveries := newStore(tx)
		recorded, err := deliveries.recordOutcome(ctx, m.id, out, now)
		if err != nil || !recorded {
			return err
		}
		pending, err := deliveries.hasPending(ctx, m.campaignID)
		if err != nil || pending || c.Status() != campaign.StatusSending {
			return err
		}
		if err := c.Finish(now); err != nil {
			return err
		}
		return campaigns.Transition(ctx, c, campaign.StatusSending)
	})
}

```
build: add the River job queue and run its migrations at startup

River v0.51.2 runs background jobs on the existing PostgreSQL pool
through its database/sql driver. database.Migrate applies River's own
versioned migrations after goose's, so the seed, the API and the test
databases get its tables; CheckQueueSchema lets a process that doesn't
migrate refuse to start on an outdated queue schema. SQLTx exposes a
GORM transaction's *sql.Tx, so jobs can be inserted in the same
transaction as the rows they belong to.

go.mod also requires golang.org/x/time, for the delivery worker's rate
limiters.
```

```
feat: dispatch campaigns and deliver them through River with mock providers

POST /api/campaigns/{id}/dispatch sends a draft now, or at scheduled_at
through River's scheduled jobs (in the future, at most a year ahead),
after checking its segment's names again. The status change and the
dispatch job are saved in one transaction, so a campaign is dispatched
only once: a concurrent or repeated request gets 409 already_dispatched.

A separate worker (cmd/worker; exactly one may run) prepares a campaign
in one transaction: a pending delivery for each recipient and channel,
each with its send job. SMS and email have their own queues, 20 workers
each and a rate limit of 100 per second that covers retries. The worker
```

```
When the user approves a proposed split (e.g. "commit" or "commit changes"), make exactly those commits.

**Re-verify right before committing:** files can change between verification and the commit. On 2026-11-14 an editor undo in the user's VS Code silently reverted six lines of an earlier Claude edit in `internal/campaign/store.go` (breaking every dispatch), seconds after the user edited another file. See [[local-check-commands]].
```

```
  type: project

Claude's non-interactive shell starts with Node v18, which fails `make check`'s `node-check`. Run frontend-touching targets as `. "$HOME/.nvm/nvm.sh" && nvm use > /dev/null && make check` (`.nvmrc` pins Node 26).

`make test-integration` refuses to run without `.env` (its `dotenv` prerequisite), and the repo may have none. Rather than creating one, pass the development address straight to the tests: `TEST_DATABASE_URL="$(sed -n 's/^DATABASE_URL=//p' .env.example)" go test -race -count=1 ./...` (needs PostgreSQL on :5432, from `make db` or the Compose stack). Never print the DSN.

**Why:** both cost a failed verification run on 2026-11-14.
```

Implement Phase 6: Frontend for the AI Campaign Composer.

Use the existing Vue 3 + TypeScript stack and backend APIs.

The goal is a modern, responsive, intuitive campaign management interface.

Keep the implementation as simple and maintainable as possible. Avoid overengineering, unnecessary abstractions, excessive components, composables, and dependencies.

1. Campaign Management

* List campaigns with their statuses and relevant details.
* Create, edit, view, and delete campaigns according to existing lifecycle rules.
* Provide clear loading, empty, and error states.

2. Campaign Wizard

Build a simple 3-step wizard:

Step 1 — Audience

* Visual segment builder supporting the existing segment DSL.
* Support AND/OR groups, nested conditions, negation, and all existing operators.
* Allow users to add, edit, and remove conditions without writing JSON.
* Show audience count/preview if supported by the backend.

Step 2 — Message

* Configure SMS and email independently.
* Support existing message placeholders.
* Show character counts and message previews.
* Apply existing validation rules.

Step 3 — Review & Dispatch

* Show a complete campaign summary.
* Allow saving drafts, immediate dispatch, and scheduling.
* Require confirmation before dispatch.

3. Delivery Monitoring

* Display campaign progress and delivery statistics.
* Show SMS and email statistics separately.
* Distinguish pending, accepted, delivered, failed, and skipped messages.
* Use the existing SSE endpoint for live updates.
* Handle reconnection, cleanup, and terminal states correctly.

4. Language Support

* Support English (EN) and Turkish (TR).
* English should be the default.
* Add a language switcher in the header.
* Persist the selected language in localStorage.
* Translate all user-facing text, including errors, dialogs, and notifications.
* Keep the i18n implementation simple.

5. UI/UX

* Modern, professional SaaS-style design.
* Responsive layout.
* Clear navigation and intuitive workflows.
* Consistent styling and accessible controls.
* Proper loading, error, success, and confirmation states.
* Avoid unnecessary animations and visual complexity.

6. Code Quality & Simplicity

Simplicity is a top priority.

* Prefer straightforward Vue Composition API code.
* Use local reactive state wherever possible.
* Avoid introducing Pinia unless genuinely necessary.
* Avoid unnecessary composables, generic components, wrappers, and abstraction layers.
* Keep API integration simple and centralized.
* Extract reusable components only when they provide real value.
* Prefer readable, explicit code over clever or highly generic solutions.
* Minimize dependencies and boilerplate.
* Follow existing project conventions.

Do not introduce architectural complexity for hypothetical future requirements.

7. Phase 7 Compatibility

Phase 7 will introduce AI-powered campaign creation and editing.

Keep the campaign wizard reusable so AI-generated drafts can later be reviewed and edited through the same interface.

Do not implement AI functionality in this phase.

8. Testing & Documentation

* Add focused tests for important user flows, validation, language switching, and SSE behavior.
* Run relevant checks and tests.
* Update existing project documentation with Phase 6 results.

Implementation Process

First, inspect the relevant existing code and prepare a concise implementation plan covering:

* Pages and components.
* Campaign wizard and segment builder.
* State management.
* API integration and any missing backend capabilities.
* Localization.
* SSE handling.
* Testing.

Do not start implementation until I approve the plan.

Prioritize the simplest solution that fully satisfies the requirements.

```
	segment, err := s.prepare(ctx, q)
```
